
**Caption.** Two commodities, two journeys, four expired figures. Everything carrying a stamp is
printed, dated and **not updated here**; the current rankings belong to two other owners and two
other questions.

### ✅ The plantation paragraph, printed

**✅ Leong printed p. 119 (scan PDF 126)**, read directly at generation:

> "**plantations have been established, especially in Java, Sumatra, Malaysia, West Africa and
> Central America.** The climate has proved to be **very favourable for the cultivation of certain
> crops that are highly valued in the industrial West**. The most …"

⚠️ **Disclosure of a damaged scan.** On scan PDF 126 the **left column carries a band of illegible
text** between *"The most …"* and *"…in the Amazon basin, it has since been transplanted"*. The lost
band evidently introduces rubber and names its Amazonian origin. **Nothing is reconstructed from
it.** The sentences quoted in this lesson are the fully legible ones, and the Amazonian origin is
taken instead from the **separately legible** statement at printed p. 118 that *"in the Amazon basin
the Indian tribes collect wild rubber"*.

⚠️ **The clause worth noticing is "highly valued in the industrial West".** Leong is stating, without
comment, that the plantation system was organised around **demand located outside the producing
region**. C1 line 73 says the same thing more carefully — *"historically expanded under
colonial/export-market conditions"* — and that is the right framing. **Plantation geography is
demand geography**, which is exactly why a regulation written in Brussels (the current anchor below)
can reshape land use on the equator.

### ✗ Correction C18 — the rubber figures, printed and stamped

**✅ Leong printed p. 119, verbatim:**

> "…in the Amazon basin, it has since been **transplanted to other parts of the equatorial lands and
> is grown very profitably on large estates. Malaysia and Indonesia are the leading producers, each
> accounting for more than a third of the world production. The home country, Brazil exports
> practically no natural rubber.** The problems of **tree diseases** and the **lack of commercial
> organization of the Indians in the Amazon lowlands** have brought about this unexpected **'shift'
> of rubber cultivation**."

| Element | Status |
|---|---|
| Rubber originates in the Amazon and was **transplanted** | ✅ printed, and **still true** — a historical fact, not a statistic |
| Grown *"very profitably on **large estates**"* | ✅ printed, and structurally still the dominant mode |
| **Malaysia and Indonesia each more than a third** of world production | ✅ printed · ⏱ **1970s-vintage** · ⚠️ **the ranking has since changed** |
| **Brazil exports practically none** | ✅ printed · ⏱ **1970s-vintage** |
| The **explanation**: tree diseases and lack of commercial organisation | ✅ printed, and a genuine historical explanation |

⚠️ **What this edition does and does not do.** It **prints** the sentence, because the sentence is
the book's and because an examiner's close option may be built from it. It **stamps** the vintage. It
**states that the ranking has changed**. And it **refuses to supply the current ranking**, because
↪ **`Geography/advanced/30` owns 2022 Mains GS-I Q15**, *"Rubber-producing countries and
environmental issues"*. A current production ranking offered from Topic 15 would be answering another
owner's question (**refusal class 2**, gate **G13**).

⚠️ **What you may safely take from the passage, because it is not a statistic.** The **mechanism of
the shift** is durable and analytically useful: a crop can leave its region of origin when
**disease pressure is high at home** — pathogens co-evolve with the host in its native range — and
when **commercial organisation is better elsewhere**. That is a transferable argument about why
plantation crops cluster away from their centres of origin, and it costs nothing in currency risk.

### ✗ Correction C17 — the cocoa figures, printed, stamped, and deliberately not answered

**✅ Leong printed p. 119, verbatim:**

> "Another tropical crop that has achieved an amazing success is **cocoa**. It is most extensively
> cultivated in **West Africa, bordering the Gulf of Guinea**. The **two most important producers
> are Ghana and Nigeria**. There is a keen demand for the crop and **acreages are rapidly on the
> increase**. Most of the crop leaves West Africa for **Europe or North America** for the cocoa and
> chocolate industry. From the same area another crop, **oil palm**, has done equally well and
> **many countries outside Africa have now taken to its cultivation**. Other crops that have been
> found suitable for the hot, wet equatorial climate and are extensively cultivated are
> **coconuts, sugar, coffee, tea, tobacco, spices, cinchona, bananas, pineapples and sago**."

> ⛔ **EXPLICIT REFUSAL, AND THE REASON FOR IT.** **2024 Prelims Q5** asks for *"World's two largest
> cocoa producers"* and is routed in the ledger to **`Geography/basic/34`**. **Topic 15 does not
> answer it.** This edition therefore does **two** things and stops: it prints Leong's sentence —
> *"The two most important producers are Ghana and Nigeria"* — with its **1970s vintage stamp** and
> a statement that **the ranking has since changed**; and it **names the owner of the question**.
> **No current cocoa production ranking appears anywhere in this file** (**refusal class 2**, gate
> **G13**). This is the clearest case in the edition of an **adjacency trap**: the sentence is in
> Topic 15's book, and the question is not Topic 15's question.

⚠️ **Two items in that paragraph are not rankings and are therefore safe and valuable.** First,
**"West Africa, bordering the Gulf of Guinea"** is a **locational** fact about where the cocoa belt
lies, and the **Gulf of Guinea has a map-bank row**. Second, the **ten-crop list** — coconuts, sugar,
coffee, tea, tobacco, spices, cinchona, bananas, pineapples, sago — is a **climatic suitability**
list, not a production list. C1 line 74 gives **four** crops; **six are dropped**, and **cinchona**
and **sago** in particular are the kind of item a close option is built from.

### 📰 Current anchor — the demand side, as regulation

📰 **Reported, 2026.** The commodity system Leong describes — equatorial production for consumption
*"highly valued in the industrial West"* — is now being reshaped from the **demand** end. The **EU
Deforestation Regulation**, which requires that listed commodities including **cocoa, rubber and palm
oil** placed on or exported from the EU market be **deforestation-free** and **traceable by
geolocation to the plot of origin** for production after 2020, has been **further postponed**:
reported application from **30 December 2026** for large and medium operators and **30 June 2027**
for small and micro enterprises, accompanied by a simplification package that concentrates
due-diligence obligations on **upstream operators** and introduces **country and regional risk
classification** (reported by multiple trade-compliance outlets, 2026).

⚠️ **Carried as regulatory status and date only, and here is the boundary.** **No production
ranking, market share, trade volume or price is taken from this reporting.** Its relevance to
Topic 15 is a single structural point that belongs squarely to this lesson: **the three commodities
the chapter names are precisely the commodities now regulated for deforestation**, which is the
clearest available demonstration that **plantation expansion and forest clearance are the same
process seen from two ends**. ↪ Deforestation-free supply chains **as a forest-governance
instrument**, alongside REDD+ and Plan Vivo, belong to `Env-Ecology/basic/12`, which owns **2021
Prelims Q24** and **2026 Prelims Q40**. ↪ Commodity markets and trade policy belong to the Economy
owners. ↪ Plantation **agronomy** belongs to `Geography/advanced/30`.

### Causal chain, assumptions and limits

```text
  equatorial climate: continuous warmth + continuous moisture + no winter
        │
        ├──► IDEAL for WOODY PERENNIALS that crop continuously
        │         │
        │         ▼
        │    rubber, cocoa, oil palm, coffee, tea, cinchona …  ✅ ten-crop list
        │
        ├──► and a PERMANENT CROP SOLVES THE LESSON-8 PROBLEM
        │         ├──► canopy retained → soil not exposed to torrential rain
        │         ├──► roots retained   → leaching losses reduced
        │         └──► ⚠️ BUT nutrient export is continuous (the crop leaves),
        │              so external inputs become structurally necessary
        │
        ├──► and a MONOCULTURE SOLVES THE LESSON-10 PROBLEM
        │         └──► ✅ "large estates": pure stands replace the "no pure
        │              stands" obstacle; harvest is concentrated, not scattered
        │              ⚠️ at the cost of disease vulnerability — which is
        │                 EXACTLY Leong's reason for rubber leaving the Amazon
        │
        ▼
  DEMAND LOCATED ELSEWHERE: ✅ "highly valued in the INDUSTRIAL WEST";
  ✅ cocoa "leaves West Africa for EUROPE or NORTH AMERICA"
        │
        ├──► ⏱ historical: colonial and export-market organisation (C1 line 73)
        │
        └──► 📰 2026: the SAME demand link now carries a REGULATORY obligation
                   back down the chain (EUDR, applying 30 Dec 2026 /
                   30 Jun 2027) ──► ↪ Env-Ecology/basic/12
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ⚠️ AND THE COST SIDE, which C1 line 89 states correctly:
     monoculture · labour systems · DEFORESTATION
        └──► plantation expansion IS forest clearance — the two are one process
```

**Assumptions.** That estate-scale organisation, capital and a labour supply are available; that
demand is external and durable; that land can be cleared and held.

**What this chain does NOT explain.** It does not explain **yields, agronomy or cropping systems** —
↪ `Geography/advanced/30`. It does not explain **commodity price formation or trade policy** — ↪
Economy owners. It does not explain **the labour history** of plantation systems, which is social
history. And it supplies **no current production ranking for any commodity**, by design.

### India hook — rubber in Kerala, oil palm in the North-East

🇮🇳 **The hook.** India grows two of the three named commodities in precisely the belts this edition
is about: **natural rubber in Kerala**, on the humid windward side of the Western Ghats, and **oil
palm** promoted in the **North-East** and in parts of the peninsula. Both sit on, or adjacent to,
India's evergreen-analogue belts, and both are the same structural response — a **woody perennial
monoculture on a permanently humid, high-rainfall slope**.

**The stated limitation.** **Indian plantations are policy-driven, not climatically inevitable.**
Kerala's rubber economy and the oil-palm expansion in the North-East exist because of **promotion,
assured procurement, pricing arrangements and area-expansion programmes**, not merely because the
rainfall suits the crop — and the areas climatically suitable are far larger than the areas planted.
Climate sets **possibility**; policy and markets set **extent**. ↪ The agronomy, the yields, the
area-expansion programmes and the commodity-trade dimension are owned by **`Geography/advanced/30`**,
which also owns **2022 Mains GS-I Q15**. This lesson names the Indian cases as **analogues of the
plantation logic** and teaches none of their agronomy.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison — three land uses on the same ground.**

| | **Primary forest** | **Shifting cultivation** | **Plantation** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Canopy | permanent, mixed | removed, then regrown as belukar | permanent, **single species** |
| Nutrient strategy | closed biomass loop ✅ | ash pulse, then fallow rebuild ✅ | **external inputs**, because the crop is exported |
| Species per unit area | ✅ *"200 … in an acre"* | few crops, then regrowth | **one** |
| Extraction economics | ✅ *"a most difficult task"* | subsistence plus some sale | concentrated, estate-scale ✅ |
| Principal vulnerability | clearance | **fallow shortening** | **disease** — ✅ Leong's own reason for rubber's shift |
| Soil exposure to torrential rain | minimal | **high during the cropping years** | low once established |

**Objection.** "Plantation agriculture is simply deforestation with a better press. The distinction
is cosmetic."

**Reply.** The objection is **half right, and the half it gets wrong matters**. It is right that
**establishing** a plantation generally means clearing forest, that C1 line 89 correctly names
deforestation among the costs, and that the 2026 regulatory anchor exists precisely because
plantation expansion and forest loss are the same process seen from two ends. It is wrong about the
**steady state**. An established plantation is a **permanent woody cover** that retains canopy and
roots, reduces soil exposure to torrential rain, and can be cropped indefinitely — which is why it
succeeds where annual cropping fails within three years. The honest formulation distinguishes the
**transition** from the **steady state**: *conversion is destructive; the converted system is
stable*. Collapsing the two produces either an apologia or a caricature, and examiners reward
neither.

**Unresolved residual.** **How much of current plantation area was converted from primary forest,
from secondary belukar, or from already-cleared land is not determinable from any source in this
topic**, and the distinction is exactly what the 2026 regulatory regime is trying to establish
through plot-level traceability. ⚠️ This edition therefore records the structural relationship and
**supplies no conversion figure, no area and no share**.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Repeating "Ghana and Nigeria" as the current cocoa leaders.** ⏱ **1970s-vintage**; the ranking
  has changed. ↪ **2024 Prelims Q5** belongs to `Geography/basic/34`.
- ❌ **Supplying a current cocoa, rubber, oil-palm or coffee ranking from this topic.** **Refusal
  class 2**, gate **G13**.
- ❌ **Repeating "Malaysia and Indonesia each more than a third" as current.** ⏱ **1970s-vintage**.
  ↪ **2022 Mains GS-I Q15** belongs to `Geography/advanced/30`.
- ❌ **Forgetting that rubber is **Amazonian** in origin.** ✅ *"the Indian tribes collect wild
  rubber"* (p. 118) and ✅ *"transplanted"* (p. 119) — the provenance is the point.
- ❌ **Giving four plantation crops.** The printed suitability list has **ten**: coconuts, sugar,
  coffee, tea, tobacco, spices, **cinchona**, bananas, pineapples, **sago**.
- ❌ **Dropping the five named estate regions.** ✅ **Java, Sumatra, Malaysia, West Africa, Central
  America**.
- ❌ **Treating plantation and deforestation as either identical or unrelated.** Distinguish the
  **transition** from the **steady state**.
- ❌ **Using the 2026 EUDR reporting for market shares or volumes.** It is carried as **regulatory
  status and date only**.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I *"Salient features of world's physical geography"* — the commercial agricultural
system that a named climatic type supports, taught as a response to the type.

**PYQ position.** **Topic 15 owns no routed PYQ for this content, and two adjacent demands are
expressly routed away and not answered here:** **2024 Prelims Q5** (world's two largest cocoa
producers) → **`Geography/basic/34`**; **2022 Mains GS-I Q15** (rubber-producing countries and
environmental issues) → **`Geography/advanced/30`**. Topic 15's own demands remain
`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` lines **453** and **666**, both `Routed; key unavailable
locally`. **No answer, key or current ranking is supplied anywhere.**

**Answer use.** Build the plantation answer on **why the system fits the environment**, then on
**provenance**, then stamp any figure you quote. *"Plantation agriculture succeeds where annual
cropping fails because woody perennials retain canopy and roots, so the soil is not exposed to
torrential rain each year, and because estate-scale monoculture replaces the forest's 'absence of
pure stands'. Leong records estates in Java, Sumatra, Malaysia, West Africa and Central America for
crops 'highly valued in the industrial West'. Provenance is instructive: rubber was collected wild
in the Amazon and 'has since been transplanted', the shift attributed to tree diseases and weak
commercial organisation at home. Producer rankings in the book are 1970s-vintage and have changed;
current rankings belong to other owners."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ Estates established *"especially in **Java, Sumatra, Malaysia, West Africa and Central
   America**"*, for crops *"**highly valued in the industrial West**"*.
2. ✅ Rubber: *"in the Amazon basin the **Indian tribes collect wild rubber**"* (p. 118) → *"it has
   since been **transplanted** … grown very profitably on **large estates**"* (p. 119).
3. ⏱ ✅ *"**Malaysia and Indonesia are the leading producers, each accounting for more than a third**
   of the world production. The home country, **Brazil exports practically no natural rubber**."*
   **1970s-vintage; the ranking has changed; the current ranking is refused here.**
4. ✅ Leong's **explanation** for the shift, which is durable: **tree diseases** and **lack of
   commercial organisation** in the Amazon lowlands.
5. ⏱ ✅ Cocoa: *"most extensively cultivated in **West Africa, bordering the Gulf of Guinea**. The
   **two most important producers are Ghana and Nigeria**."* **1970s-vintage.** ⛔ **2024 Prelims Q5
   is routed to `Geography/basic/34` and is not answered here.**
6. ✅ *"acreages are **rapidly on the increase**"*; the crop *"leaves West Africa for **Europe or
   North America**"*.
7. ⏱ ✅ Oil palm: *"from the same area … **many countries outside Africa** have now taken to its
   cultivation."*
8. ✅ **The complete ten-crop suitability list:** **coconuts, sugar, coffee, tea, tobacco, spices,
   cinchona, bananas, pineapples, sago**. C1 gives four.
9. ⚠️ **Why plantations work where cropping fails:** permanent woody cover retains canopy and roots
   (Lesson 8); monoculture replaces *"no pure stands"* (Lesson 10) — at the cost of **disease
   vulnerability**, which is Leong's own reason for rubber's departure from the Amazon.
10. 📰 **2026:** EU Deforestation Regulation covering **cocoa, rubber and palm oil** reported further
    postponed to **30 December 2026** (large and medium operators) and **30 June 2027** (small and
    micro), with plot-level geolocation traceability for post-2020 production — carried as
    **regulatory status and date only**. ↪ `Env-Ecology/basic/12`.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why plantation agriculture succeeds in the equatorial environment where annual
cropping fails within a few years, and state the vulnerability that this solution creates.

**Model answer:** Because plantation crops are **woody perennials**. They retain canopy and roots
permanently, so the soil is not exposed to torrential rain each season and leaching losses are
limited — unlike a cleared ladang, where *"the torrential downpours soon wash out most of the soil
nutrients"* within two or three crops. Estate-scale **monoculture** also replaces the forest's
*"absence of pure stands"*, concentrating the harvest that scattered natural stems make
uneconomic. The vulnerability created is **disease**: a single-species stand is exposed to a single
pathogen, which is precisely Leong's explanation for rubber's departure from its Amazonian home —
*"the problems of tree diseases"*. *(105 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That plantation success proves equatorial soils are fertile. It proves
the opposite: the system works by **not disturbing** the surface and by importing nutrients, which
is a way of avoiding the soil problem rather than solving it. *(44 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 11

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Discuss the plantation economy of the hot, wet
equatorial regions, and explain why figures given for it in standard texts require dating.

**Model:** The plantation system exploits the one thing the climate does supremely well — growing
**woody perennials continuously**. Permanent cover retains canopy and roots, avoiding the clearance
sequence that destroys annual cropping, while estate monoculture overcomes the forest's *"absence of
pure stands"*. Leong records estates *"especially in Java, Sumatra, Malaysia, West Africa and Central
America"* for crops *"highly valued in the industrial West"*, listing **coconuts, sugar, coffee, tea,
tobacco, spices, cinchona, bananas, pineapples and sago** besides rubber, cocoa and oil palm.
**Dating is essential because the crops travelled.** Rubber was gathered wild in the Amazon and *"has
since been transplanted"*, the shift attributed to *"tree diseases"* and weak commercial
organisation. Leong's producer statements — **Malaysia and Indonesia each above a third; cocoa led by
Ghana and Nigeria** — are 1970s-vintage, and such rankings move with disease, policy and price. A
figure without a vintage is unusable. *(149 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Perennial-cover and monoculture logic **3** · estate regions and the full crop
list **2** · rubber provenance and Leong's stated reason for the shift **3** · vintage discipline and
refusal to update rankings **2**


Progress: 12 / 19 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **analogue-versus-instance Venn, with the failing criterion marked**

### The intuition, before any term

This lesson is the **hinge** of the edition. Everything before it was about a climatic type that
**does not occur in India**. Everything after it is about Indian forests. The join has to be made
honestly, and there is exactly one honest way to make it.

The temptation is to say: India's Western Ghats are very wet, they have tall evergreen forest, so
they are *basically* equatorial. That is wrong, and it is wrong in a way an examiner can test in a
single close option. India's wettest places are wet **seasonally**. The equatorial type is defined
by rain in **every** month. A place can receive **three times** the equatorial annual total and
still fail the test outright.

So what **does** transfer? Go back to Lesson 4. There were four rain-making mechanisms. India
shares exactly **one** of them — **orographic rain** — and shares it only for part of the year. But
that one mechanism, operating hard enough and long enough on a windward slope, produces **enough
moisture for enough of the year** that the forest never has to drop its leaves. **The forest
transfers; the climate does not.** That is the analogue, stated precisely, and it is the only form
of the claim that survives contact with the evidence.

**Plain words before the terms.** An *analogue* is something that **resembles** another thing in
specified respects; an *instance* is a **case of** that thing. India's evergreen forests are an
analogue of the selva, not an instance of it. A *disanalogy* is the respect in which the resemblance
**fails**, and naming it is what separates an argument from a slogan.

### The primary visual — the analogue Venn

```text
      WHAT TRANSFERS FROM THE EQUATOR TO INDIA, AND WHAT DOES NOT

   ┌─────────────────────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
   │   HOT WET EQUATORIAL TYPE       │   │   INDIA'S WET EVERGREEN BELTS   │
   │   (Lessons 1–11)                │   │   (Lessons 13–18)               │
   │                                 │   │                                 │
   │ ✗ rain in EVERY month           │   │ ✗ STRONGLY SEASONAL rainfall    │
   │   ✅ "There is NO MONTH          │   │   SW monsoon concentration;     │
   │   WITHOUT RAIN" (Leong p.117)   │   │   a pronounced drier season     │
   │                                 │   │                                 │
   │ ✗ annual T range 0.4–1.8 °C     │   │ ✗ markedly larger annual range  │
   │   (7 printed stations)          │   │   even at Port Blair            │
   │                                 │   │                                 │
   │ ✗ TWO rainfall maxima,          │   │ ✗ ONE dominant maximum          │
   │   April & October               │   │   (SW monsoon)                  │
   │                                 │   │                                 │
   │ ✗ DOLDRUM/ITCZ convergence      │   │ ✗ ITCZ present but as a         │
   │   overhead ~all year            │   │   MONSOON control — it ARRIVES  │
   │                                 │   │   and DEPARTS  ↪ Topic 16       │
   │ ✗ latitude 0°–10° / 5°–10°      │   │ ✗ mostly 8°–28° N               │
   │          ╲                      │   │                 ╱               │
   │           ╲    ┌────────────────┴───┴────────────────┐╱               │
   │            ╲   │   ★ THE SHARED REGION ★             │                │
   │             ╲  │                                     │                │
   │              ╲ │ ✅ OROGRAPHIC / RELIEF RAIN         │ ╱              │
   │               ╲│    (Leong p.117, Arrow 3, Lesson 4) │╱               │
   │                │                                     │                │
   │                │ ✅ ENOUGH MOISTURE FOR ENOUGH OF    │                │
   │                │    THE YEAR → NO LEAF-DROP          │                │
   │                │    → EVERGREEN HABIT                │                │
   │                │                                     │                │
   │                │ ✅ CONTINUOUS WARMTH (lowland        │                │
   │                │    tropical temperatures)           │                │
   │                │                                     │                │
   │                │ ✅ TIERED ARCHITECTURE              │                │
   │                │    Leong 150 ft = 45.7 m            │                │
   │                │    Khullar "often reach 45 metres"  │                │
   │                │    ◄ agreement to within 0.5 m      │                │
   │                │                                     │                │
   │                │ ✅ SAME LIFE-FORM SPECTRUM          │                │
   │                │    hardwoods · canes/rattans ·      │                │
   │                │    bamboos · ferns · climbers ·     │                │
   │                │    orchids as epiphytes             │                │
   │                │                                     │                │
   │                │ ✅ LEACHING REGIME + biomass-held   │                │
   │                │    nutrient capital (Lesson 8)      │                │
   │                │                                     │                │
   │                │ ✅ SAME EXTRACTION OBSTACLE:        │                │
   │                │    "absence of pure stands"         │                │
   │                │    printed in ALL THREE BOOKS       │                │
   │                └────────────────┬───┬────────────────┘                │
   └─────────────────────────────────┘   └─────────────────────────────────┘

   ╔═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
   ║  THE SHARED REGION CONTAINS NOT ONE CLIMATIC CRITERION.               ║
   ║  It contains a MECHANISM (orographic rain), an OUTCOME (evergreen     ║
   ║  habit), a STRUCTURE, a LIFE-FORM SPECTRUM, a SOIL REGIME and an      ║
   ║  ECONOMICS.  That is why the analogue is strong AND why calling it    ║
   ║  an instance is false.                                                ║
   ╚═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
   ✗ AND NOT ONE GENUS IS SHARED.  (Lesson 7)
```

**Caption.** The overlap is large, substantive and entirely **non-climatic**. Every item in the two
outer columns is a **climatic** criterion, and India fails all of them.

### ✅ C2's disclaimer, preserved — and why it is a strength

**✅ C2 lines 12–15, quoted as the owner's own framing:**

> "India's dominant humid-tropical climate is monsoonal, but some modern Köppen maps classify
> limited island or very wet pockets as **Af**. **The safer distinction is that India's evergreen
> forests are shaped by monsoon seasonality, orography and insularity.** Key belts are the windward
> Western Ghats, North-East India and the Andaman-Nicobar Islands."

**✅ And C2 line 24:** *"India has **equatorial-type evergreen forests**, not a true equatorial
climate zone."*

⚠️ **This is one of the three preserved strengths of the owner pair (gate G16), and it is preserved
here in substance and improved only in two respects.** First, the **reason** for the disclaimer is
supplied — the no-dry-month criterion — which C2 states as a conclusion without the evidence.
Second, C2's hedge about *"some modern Köppen maps"* is **carried as C2's hedge and not converted
into a claim by this edition**, because ↪ **the Köppen criterion, its letters, its thresholds and
the mapping of Indian Köppen regions are owned by `Geography/basic/14` and `Geography/advanced/14`**.
Topic 15 is not the owner of the classification question and does not adjudicate it. What Topic 15
says is the **vegetation** statement: India has equatorial-**type** evergreen forest.

### ✅ The disanalogy, proved from the books rather than asserted

**The defining criterion, ✅ Leong printed p. 117:** *"**There is no month without rain**, and a
distinct dry season like those of the Savanna or the Tropical Monsoon Climates, is absent."*

⚠️ Note that **Leong names the Tropical Monsoon Climate explicitly as the contrast case**. The book
itself sets India on the other side of the line — and at printed p. 120 it makes the comparison a
**student exercise**, printing graphs for ✅ *"**Alor Star in West Malaysia** and **Bombay in
India**"* and asking the reader to identify *"two different types of climate"*. **The chapter's own
exercise is the India disanalogy.**

**And the margin stations, ✅ Leong printed p. 117:** *"**Kota Bharu, Kelantan** receives most of its
rainfall from the **North-East Monsoon** at the end of the year and **Rangoon, Burma**, from the
**South-West Monsoon** between June and September."* Kota Bharu is at about 6° N — **inside both of
Leong's latitude envelopes** — and it is **not** of the equatorial type, because its rainfall is
seasonal. ★ **This is the single cleanest proof available that latitude does not decide the type**,
and it comes from Leong's own page.

### ✅ The Khullar ladder — how India actually sorts its vegetation

If India is not sorted by Köppen letters, how is its vegetation sorted? ✅ **Khullar folio 168 (scan
PDF 101 left)**, read directly at generation, answers in one paragraph — and the audit was right to
call it the most answer-useful paragraph in the chapter:

> "The amount of annual rainfall has a great bearing on the type of vegetation. Areas receiving
> **200 cm or more** rainfall per annum have stands of **evergreen rain forests** while **monsoon
> deciduous forests** dominate in areas with rainfall between **100 and 200 cm**. In areas having
> **50 to 100 cm** rainfall there are **drier deciduous or tropical savana** grading into **open
> thorny scrub** while those with **less than 50 cm** rainfall have only **dry thorny scrub and low
> open bush merging into semi desert**. In **higher altitudes of the Himalayas and the hills of the
> Peninsula at elevation of more than 900 metre** above sea level, **temperature plays a more
> important role**."

```text
   KHULLAR'S INDIAN RAINFALL LADDER — folio 168, verbatim thresholds
   cm/yr
   ─────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
        │
   250+ │  ████████  (folio 169's Wet Evergreen threshold — SEE LESSON 14)
        │
   200  ├──────────  ✅ "200 cm or more … EVERGREEN RAIN FORESTS"
        │  ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓
   100  ├──────────  ✅ "100 and 200 cm … MONSOON DECIDUOUS FORESTS"
        │  ▒▒▒▒▒▒▒▒
    50  ├──────────  ✅ "50 to 100 cm … DRIER DECIDUOUS OR TROPICAL SAVANA
        │  ░░░░░░░░     grading into OPEN THORNY SCRUB"
     0  └──────────  ✅ "less than 50 cm … DRY THORNY SCRUB AND LOW OPEN
                        BUSH MERGING INTO SEMI DESERT"

   AND A SEPARATE AXIS:
   ✅ above 900 m in the Himalayas and the Peninsular hills,
      "TEMPERATURE PLAYS A MORE IMPORTANT ROLE"   ──► ↪ Geography/advanced/22
```

⚠️ **Read what this ladder is and is not.** It is **not** a climatic classification. It is a
**vegetation-response ladder keyed to a single variable**, annual rainfall, with a **second axis**
(altitude, above 900 m) switching the controlling variable to temperature. **India's vegetation is
sorted by rainfall amount; the equatorial type is defined by rainfall distribution.** Two different
organising principles, which is exactly why the transfer must be made at the level of **forest**
rather than **climate**.

⚠️ And note the **tension already visible**: folio 168 puts evergreen rain forest above **200 cm**,
while folio 169 — the very next printed page — requires Tropical Wet Evergreen to exceed
**250 cm**. **That conflict is named and left unresolved at Lesson 14**, and it is flagged here so
that the ladder is not learned as settled.

### Causal chain, assumptions and limits

```text
  INDIA: seasonal monsoon circulation, NOT an equatorial convergence regime
        │
        ├──► ✗ no year-round overhead convergence
        ├──► ✗ no two-maxima annual curve
        ├──► ✗ no 0.4–1.8 °C annual temperature range
        └──► ✗ THEREFORE NOT THE EQUATORIAL TYPE  (criterion-level failure)
        │
        ▼
  BUT: SW monsoon flow + a steep, high, continuous windward barrier
        │
        ▼
  ✅ OROGRAPHIC RAIN (Leong's Arrow 3) operating at extreme intensity
        │
        ├──► very large annual totals on the windward side
        │
        └──► and — decisively — the WET PERIOD IS LONG ENOUGH, and the
             dry period SHORT ENOUGH, that trees need not shed to survive it
                  │
                  ▼
        ✅ Khullar folio 169: "the dry season is DISTINCTLY SHORT"
                  │
                  ▼
        ✅ "Due to high heat and high humidity, the trees of these forests
           DO NOT SHED THEIR LEAVES ANNUALLY, AT LEAST NOT TOGETHER, and
           are termed as EVERGREEN FORESTS"
                  │
                  ▼
        EVERGREEN HABIT  ──►  and with it the whole shared region:
        tiered architecture · life-form spectrum · leaching regime ·
        biomass-held nutrients · "absence of pure stands" economics
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ✗ WHAT DOES NOT COME WITH IT: the flora (not one shared genus),
    the climate criteria, the two-maxima curve, the thermal equability.
```

⚠️ **Khullar's definition of "evergreen" is worth pausing on, because it is subtler than the word
suggests.** ✅ Folio 169: the trees *"**do not shed their leaves annually, at least not together**"*.
Evergreen does **not** mean that individual leaves are permanent; it means that **leaf fall is not
synchronised into a season**, so the canopy is never bare. That is the same **absence of a seasonal
synchroniser** that Leong describes for the selva at printed p. 118 — *"seeding, flowering, fruiting
and decaying do not take place in a seasonal pattern"*. **Two books, two continents, one definition
of evergreenness: de-synchronisation, not permanence.** That is the deepest point of correspondence
between the two halves of this edition.

**Assumptions.** A windward position relative to the monsoon flow; sufficient barrier height and
continuity; a dry season short enough not to force synchronised leaf fall.

**What this chain does NOT explain.** It does not explain **why the monsoon exists or when it
arrives** — ↪ `Geography/basic/16`. It does not explain **where the Köppen boundaries fall in India**
— ↪ `Geography/basic/14`. And it does not explain **insularity**, C2's third named control, whose
island geography is ↪ `Geography/advanced/11`.

### India hook — Mawsynram and Cherrapunji, the instructive failures

🇮🇳 **The hook.** **Mawsynram** and **Cherrapunji (Sohra)** in Meghalaya are India's — and among the
world's — wettest stations, and ✅ C2 line 28 identifies the wettest belts with them. Their annual
totals **far exceed** anything printed for the equatorial type: Leong's band is **60–100 inches
(152–254 cm)** and Kuala Lumpur records **95 inches (241 cm)**, while these stations receive
multiples of that. They are the obvious candidates if one believes that *very wet* means
*equatorial*.

**The stated limitation — and this is why they are the right hook rather than the wrong one.**
**They fail the Af no-dry-month criterion outright, and they fail it worse than less rainy places
do.** Their enormous totals are delivered in a **highly concentrated** monsoon season against a
pronounced dry period; the regime is the **most seasonal** imaginable, not the least. ⚠️ So
Mawsynram and Cherrapunji demonstrate the lesson's central discipline better than any other Indian
example: **total and distribution are independent variables, and the type is defined on
distribution.** A station can hold a world rainfall record and be **further** from the equatorial
type than a modest lowland station with rain in every month. ⚠️ **Map-bank rows absent** for both
Mawsynram and Cherrapunji/Sohra; located here by description only (**H18**, gate **G25**).

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Calling India's evergreen forests equatorial.** They are **equatorial-type** — an analogue.
  ✅ C2 line 24 says so and this edition preserves it.
- ❌ **Using very high rainfall totals as evidence of the type.** Mawsynram and Cherrapunji have
  **enormous totals and extreme seasonality**; the criterion is **no dry month**.
- ❌ **Adjudicating whether any Indian pocket is Köppen Af from this topic.** ↪ The criterion and the
  Indian Köppen mapping belong to `Geography/basic/14` and `Geography/advanced/14`. C2's *"some
  modern Köppen maps"* is carried **as C2's hedge**, not converted into a claim.
- ❌ **Transferring the flora with the structure.** ✗ **Not one genus** is shared (Lesson 7).
- ❌ **Forgetting that Kota Bharu is inside the latitude band and outside the type.** It is the
  cleanest printed proof that latitude is not the criterion.
- ❌ **Reading Khullar's folio 168 ladder as settled.** ⇄ Folio 169 sets the evergreen threshold at
  **250 cm**, not 200 — **Lesson 14**.
- ❌ **Thinking "evergreen" means leaves are permanent.** ✅ Khullar: trees *"do not shed their leaves
  annually, **at least not together**"* — de-synchronisation, not permanence.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, the India-applied limb — *"distribution of key natural resources across … the
Indian subcontinent"* in its vegetation aspect, and the disciplined relation of an Indian formation
to a world climatic type.

**PYQ position.** **Topic 15 owns no routed PYQ for this content; this lesson supports
`Geography/basic/14_Climate-Classification-Koppen`** (the Af criterion and Indian Köppen regions)
and **`Geography/basic/16`** (the monsoon seasonality that defeats the analogue, and which owns
**2026 Prelims Q33**). **C2 owns no routed PYQ anywhere in 2018–2026**, confirmed at
`PYQ-INTEGRATION-AUDIT-2018-2023.md` line **636**, `...2024-2025.md` line **352** and `...2026.md`
line **420**. Topic 15's own two demands remain at `_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` lines **453**
and **666**, both `Routed; key unavailable locally`. **No key is inferred anywhere.**

**Answer use.** Open any India–equatorial comparison with the **disclaimer**, then the **mechanism**,
then the **list of what transfers**. *"India has no true equatorial climate: its humid-tropical
regime is monsoonal, and the defining criterion of the type — 'there is no month without rain' —
fails even at Mawsynram and Cherrapunji, whose enormous totals are extremely seasonal. What India
shares is a single mechanism, orographic rain, operating hard enough that the dry season is
'distinctly short', so trees 'do not shed their leaves annually, at least not together'. From that
follow the evergreen habit, the tiered architecture, the life-form spectrum, the leaching regime and
the same extraction economics — but not one shared genus."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **C2 lines 12–15, preserved:** India's humid-tropical climate is **monsoonal**; its evergreen
   forests are shaped by *"**monsoon seasonality, orography and insularity**"*.
2. ✅ **C2 line 24, preserved:** India has *"**equatorial-type evergreen forests, not a true
   equatorial climate zone**"*.
3. ⚠️ C2's hedge about *"some modern Köppen maps"* is carried **as C2's hedge**. ↪ The Köppen
   criterion and Indian Köppen mapping are **owned by Topic 14** and not adjudicated here.
4. ✅ The criterion India fails: *"**There is no month without rain**"* (Leong p. 117).
5. ★ ✅ **Kota Bharu, Kelantan** is at about **6° N** — inside both of Leong's latitude envelopes —
   and receives its rain from the **North-East Monsoon**. **Latitude is not the criterion.**
6. ✅ Leong's own exercise at printed p. 120 contrasts **Alor Star** with **Bombay** as *"two
   different types of climate"* — **the chapter sets the India disanalogy itself**.
7. ✅ **Khullar folio 168 ladder:** **>200 cm** evergreen rain forest · **100–200 cm** monsoon
   deciduous · **50–100 cm** drier deciduous or tropical savana grading into open thorny scrub ·
   **<50 cm** dry thorny scrub and low open bush merging into semi desert. Above **900 m** in the
   Himalayas and Peninsular hills, *"temperature plays a more important role"*.
8. ★ ✅ **Khullar's definition of evergreen:** trees *"**do not shed their leaves annually, at least
   not together**"* — **de-synchronisation, not permanence** — which is the same absence of a
   seasonal synchroniser Leong describes for the selva.

### Concept check

**Question:** Mawsynram receives several times the annual rainfall of Kuala Lumpur. Does this make
its climate equatorial? Justify your answer with the defining criterion.

**Model answer:** No. The hot, wet equatorial type is defined on **distribution**, not total:
Leong's criterion is that *"there is no month without rain, and a distinct dry season … is absent"*.
Mawsynram's enormous total is delivered in a **highly concentrated monsoon season** against a
pronounced dry period, so it fails the criterion **more severely** than less rainy places. Leong's
own evidence settles it: **Kota Bharu**, at about 6° N and inside his latitude band, is excluded
because its rain comes from the **North-East Monsoon**. India's evergreen forests are therefore an
**analogue**: they share orographic moisture, evergreen habit and architecture, not the climate.
*(104 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a wetter place is necessarily closer to the equatorial type. Total
and distribution are independent; a record-holding station with a sharp dry season is further from
the type than a modest station with rain every month. *(43 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 12

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "India's evergreen forests are an analogue of the
equatorial rain forest, not an instance of it." Examine.

**Model:** The statement is correct and its two halves rest on different evidence. **Not an
instance**, because India fails the defining criterion. Leong requires that *"there is no month
without rain"*; India's regime is monsoonal and seasonal, and even **Mawsynram** and
**Cherrapunji**, with far larger totals than Leong's **60–100 inches**, have a pronounced dry
season. Leong's own **Kota Bharu**, at about 6° N, is excluded for exactly this reason, and his
exercise contrasts **Alor Star** with **Bombay** as different climatic types. **An analogue**,
because one mechanism does transfer — **orographic rain**, operating hard enough that Khullar
records the dry season as *"distinctly short"* and the trees as not shedding *"annually, at least
not together"*. From that follow evergreen habit, tiered architecture — Leong's **150 feet** against
Khullar's **45 metres** — the life-form spectrum and the leaching regime. Not one genus is shared.
*(149 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Criterion-level failure proved from the printed criterion **3** · Kota Bharu
or the Alor Star–Bombay exercise used as evidence **2** · the transferring mechanism named **2** ·
the shared region itemised **2** · flora disanalogy stated **1**

## Lesson 13 — Champion and Seth: from 116 types to 5 groups and 16 sub-types

Progress: 13 / 19 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **classification tree with the five authorities on the trunk**

### The intuition, before any term

You cannot talk about India's evergreen forests without a **scheme** that says what an Indian forest
type **is**, and both owners use the type names while never naming the scheme. That is like using
Köppen's letters without saying there is a Köppen.

Why does it matter? Because **a forest type is not a natural object**. It is a **class in a
classification**, defined by stated criteria, created by named people, at a stated date, for a
stated purpose. "Tropical Wet Evergreen" is not a thing you can walk into and identify the way you
identify a river; it is a **box** with thresholds on its edges — and in the next lesson you will see
that the man who drew the box recorded two different thresholds for it on consecutive printed pages.

So this lesson does three things. It names the **authorities and their dates**. It lays out the
**whole frame** — five groups, sixteen sub-types — so that the two types this edition owns can be
seen in their place rather than in isolation. And it attaches **Table 6.1's areas** so that the
relative size of each box is visible.

**Plain words before the terms.** *Moist* and *dry* in this scheme are about **water availability**;
*tropical*, *sub-tropical*, *temperate* and *alpine* are about **temperature, which in India mostly
means altitude**. So the scheme is a **two-axis grid**: a moisture axis and a thermal-altitudinal
axis. Once you see that, the sixteen sub-types stop being a list to memorise and become a map.

### The primary visual — the classification tree

**Caption.** Five authorities, five groups, sixteen sub-types. The two this edition owns are the
first two sub-types of the first group, and together they are a modest share of a forest-type total
that is itself only part of India.

### ✅ The lineage, printed

**✅ Khullar folio 169 (scan PDF 101 right)**, read directly at generation:

> "A great variety of vegetation is found in different parts of India due to **unequal distribution
> of rainfall and temperature** as well as their **seasonal variation**, besides **varied edaphic and
> biotic conditions**. A generalised classification of Indian forests is, therefore, a difficult job.
> Various systematic classifications have been made by a number of botanists and ecologists.
> **H.G. Champion (1936) distinguished 116 types of vegetation in India**, some with further
> sub-divisions. **Champion's classification has been much simplified by G.S. Puri (1960), Legris
> (1963), Champion and S.K. Seth (1968) and S.S. Negi (1990).** Based on the suggestions of these
> scholars **India's vegetation can be divided into 5 main types and 16 sub-types** as per details
> given below:"

| ✅ Authority | ✅ Date | ✅ Contribution as printed |
|---|---:|---|
| **H.G. Champion** | **1936** | Distinguished **116 types**, some with further sub-divisions |
| **G.S. Puri** | **1960** | Simplification |
| **Legris** | **1963** | Simplification |
| **Champion and S.K. Seth** | **1968** | Simplification — **the scheme in standard use** |
| **S.S. Negi** | **1990** | Simplification |

⚠️ **Four disciplines follow from that table and each is a mark.**

1. **116 → 16 is a simplification, not a discovery.** The number of Indian forest types is a
   **decision about resolution**. Champion's 116 did not become wrong; they became **unwieldy**.
2. **The standard attribution is "Champion and Seth, 1968".** It is **not** "Champion 1936", which
   is the 116-type work, and it is **not** FSI. ⚠️ **No invented officialdom**: there is no "FSI
   forest types of India" classification in any retrieved source, and none is asserted here (gate
   **G26**).
3. **The scheme is 1968 and current official mapping may differ.** ⚠️ **U4: no current official
   type-wise area table was retrieved at audit or at generation.** That gap is **declared, not
   filled**.
4. **The criteria are printed too** — *"unequal distribution of rainfall and temperature as well as
   their seasonal variation, besides varied **edaphic and biotic** conditions"*. **Seasonality is in
   the criteria**, which is why an Indian scheme can accommodate forests that no equatorial scheme
   would recognise.

### ✅ Table 6.1 — the areas, with its denominator stated in advance

**✅ Khullar Table 6.1, folio 173 (scan PDF 103 right), *"India : Area under Forests"*, Source:
K.P. Sagreiya, *Forests and Forestry* (1994), p. 67**, read directly at generation. The printed
column header is **"Percentage of total area"** — see **X5**, and see **Lesson 16** before using
this table for anything.

| Sl. | ✅ Forest Group | ✅ m ha | ✅ % |
|---:|---|---:|---:|
| 1 | **Tropical Wet Evergreen** | **5.120** | **8.0** |
| 2 | **Tropical Semi-evergreen** | **2.624** | **4.1** |
| 3 | Tropical Moist Deciduous | 23.680 | 37.0 |
| 4 | Littoral and Swamp | 0.384 | 0.6 |
| 5 | Tropical Dry Evergreen | 0.128 | 0.2 |
| 6 | Tropical Dry Deciduous | 18.304 | 28.6 |
| 7 | Tropical Thorn | 1.664 | 2.6 |
| 8 | Sub-tropical Broad-leaved | 0.256 | 0.4 |
| 9 | Sub-tropical Moist Hill (pine) | 4.224 | 6.6 |
| 10 | Sub-tropical Dry Evergreen | 1.600 | 2.5 |
| 11 | Montane Wet Temperate | 2.304 | 3.6 |
| 12 | Himalayan Moist Temperate | 2.176 | 3.4 |
| 13 | Himalayan Dry Temperate | 0.192 | 0.3 |
| 14 | Alpine (incl. sub-alpine, moist and dry alpine scrub) | 1.344 | 2.1 |
| | **Total** | **64.000** | **100.0** |

**Arithmetic verified at generation**, independently of the audit: the fourteen areas sum to
**exactly 64.000**; every percentage equals **area ÷ 64 rounded to one decimal**, with **no row
deviating**; the percentages sum to **exactly 100.0**. ⚠️ And the denominator test that settles
**X5**: **5.120 ÷ 64 = 8.0 %**, while **5.120 ÷ 328.7 = 1.56 %**. The column is a share of the
**64 m ha forest-type total**, not of India.

⚠️ **What the table tells you at a glance, and it is not what people expect.** India's forest estate
is **overwhelmingly deciduous**: sub-types 3 and 6 together are **41.984 m ha = 65.6 %** of the
column total. The two types this edition owns are **7.744 m ha = 12.1 %**. **The evergreen forest is
the small, famous part of a largely deciduous country.** ↪ The deciduous types themselves belong to
`Geography/basic/17` and `Geography/advanced/17`.

### ✅ The groups this edition does not own, named once and routed

Group boundaries are only useful if you know where they are, so each is stated once with its
printed detail and then routed.

| Group | ✅ Printed defining detail (Khullar folios 169–173) | Routed to |
|---|---|---|
| **B. Dry Tropical** | sub-types 5 Tropical Dry Evergreen, 6 Tropical Dry Deciduous, 7 Tropical Thorn | `Geography/basic/17`, `Geography/advanced/17` |
| **C. Montane Sub-tropical** | 8 Sub-tropical broad leaved hill, 9 Sub-tropical moist hill (**pine**), 10 Sub-tropical dry evergreen | `Geography/advanced/22` |
| **D. Montane Temperate** | 11 **Montane Wet Temperate**: **1800–3000 m**, rainfall **150–300 cm**, mean annual temperature **11–14 °C**, relative humidity **over 80 %**; higher hills of **Tamil Nadu and Kerala** and the Eastern Himalaya **east of 88° E**; trees rarely above **6 m**. 12 **Himalayan Moist Temperate**: **1500–3300 m**, rainfall **150–250 cm**, conifers **30–50 m**, deodar dominant west of **80° E** where rainfall is **115–180 cm**. 13 **Himalayan Dry Temperate**: precipitation **below 100 cm, mostly snow** | `Geography/advanced/22` |
| **E. Alpine** | **2,900–3,500 m**, locally to **3,800 m**; sub-alpine conifers about **30 m** against broad-leaved **10 m**; moist alpine scrub from **3,000 m** to the snowline; dry alpine scrub **above 3,500 m** | `Geography/advanced/22` |

⚠️ **Why these are listed at all rather than simply omitted.** Two reasons. First, **a classification
is unusable in pieces**: a candidate who knows only sub-types 1 and 2 cannot say what they are *not*,
and close options in this area are built on adjacent classes. Second, the numbers above are the
**boundary markers** — 1800 m, 3000 m, 88° E, 80° E, 3,500 m — and recognising that a question has
crossed one of them is how you know it is not your question. **They are named here and developed
nowhere in this file.**

### Causal chain, assumptions and limits

```text
  INDIA: enormous range of rainfall, temperature, seasonality, soil and biota
        │
        ▼
  ✅ "A generalised classification of Indian forests is … a DIFFICULT JOB"
        │
        ▼
  ✅ CHAMPION 1936: 116 TYPES — high resolution, low usability
        │
        │  simplification by PURI 1960, LEGRIS 1963,
        │  CHAMPION & SETH 1968, NEGI 1990
        ▼
  ✅ 5 MAIN TYPES, 16 SUB-TYPES — the working scheme
        │
        ├── axis 1: MOISTURE   ──► A moist tropical / B dry tropical
        │
        └── axis 2: THERMAL (in India mostly ALTITUDE)
                   ──► tropical / sub-tropical / temperate / alpine
        │
        ▼
  ⚠️ A FOREST TYPE IS A CLASS WITH THRESHOLDS ON ITS EDGES
        │
        ├──► the thresholds are rainfall, temperature and humidity values
        │         ──► LESSON 14, where two of them conflict
        │
        └──► each class has an AREA, measured at a DATE, by a METHOD
                  ──► ✅ Table 6.1, Sagreiya ⏱1994, 64.000 m ha
                  ──► ⚠️ U4: NO CURRENT OFFICIAL TYPE-WISE TABLE RETRIEVED
                  ──► LESSON 16, where four quantities are held apart
```

**Assumptions.** That a continuous vegetation gradient can be usefully partitioned; that the
criteria are stably measurable; that the 1968 classes still correspond to ground conditions after
six decades of land-use change.

**What this chain does NOT explain.** It does not explain **how Champion derived 116 types**, which
Khullar does not state. It does not supply a **current official type-wise area** — ⚠️ **U4**,
declared and not filled. And it does not describe **groups C, D and E** beyond their boundary
markers, which are ↪ `Geography/advanced/22`.

### India hook — the sixteen sub-types as India's vegetation grammar

🇮🇳 **The hook.** The sixteen sub-types are the **grammar** of any Indian vegetation answer. Given a
place, you can locate it on two axes — how wet, and how high — and name the class; given a class,
you can state its rainfall, temperature and humidity signature. ✅ Khullar's own group D entries show
the form: *Montane Wet Temperate*, **1800–3000 m**, **150–300 cm**, **11–14 °C**, humidity **over
80 %**. Every class in the scheme is specified that way, and the two this edition owns are specified
that way at **Lesson 14**.

**The stated limitation.** **The scheme is 1968 and current official mapping may differ.** ⚠️ **U4
is live**: no current official Champion–Seth type-wise area table was retrieved either at audit or at
generation, and Table 6.1's areas are a **Sagreiya 1994** datum reported in a textbook, not a current
government return. Use the **scheme** confidently as a framework and the **areas** only with their
vintage attached. ⚠️ And **no official designation is invented**: there is no "FSI forest types"
scheme asserted anywhere in this file (gate **G26**).

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Attributing the 16-sub-type scheme to "Champion 1936".** 1936 is the **116-type** work. The
  standard scheme is **Champion and Seth, 1968**.
- ❌ **Dropping Puri (1960), Legris (1963) and Negi (1990).** All three are printed in the lineage.
- ❌ **Calling it an FSI classification.** ⚠️ **No such designation appears in any retrieved source**
  (gate **G26**).
- ❌ **Treating "116 types" as superseded error.** It was **simplified**, not refuted — a resolution
  decision.
- ❌ **Reading Table 6.1's percentages as shares of India's area.** The printed header says
  *"Percentage of total area"*, and the **arithmetic proves** the denominator is **64.000 m ha**
  (**X5**, **Lesson 16**).
- ❌ **Quoting Table 6.1 without its vintage.** It is **Sagreiya, 1994**, reported in Khullar.
- ❌ **Assuming India is mainly evergreen.** Sub-types 3 and 6 are **65.6 %** of the column total;
  the two evergreen types are **12.1 %**.
- ❌ **Teaching groups C, D or E.** ↪ `Geography/advanced/22`. Named here, developed nowhere.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"distribution of key natural resources across … the Indian subcontinent"* in its
vegetation limb — the classification frame within which India's forest resources are distributed.

**PYQ position.** **Topic 15 owns no routed PYQ for this content; this lesson supports
`Geography/advanced/22`** for the montane and alpine groups and **`Geography/basic/17` and
`Geography/advanced/17`** for the deciduous and thorn types. **C2 owns no routed PYQ in 2018–2026**
(`PYQ-INTEGRATION-AUDIT-2018-2023.md` line 636; `...2024-2025.md` line 352; `...2026.md` line 420).
Topic 15's own demands remain at `_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` lines **453** and **666**, both
`Routed; key unavailable locally`. **No key is inferred anywhere.**

**Answer use.** Name the scheme before you use its vocabulary, and give the lineage in one clause.
*"India's forests are classified on the Champion–Seth scheme: H.G. Champion distinguished 116 types
in 1936, simplified by Puri (1960), Legris (1963), Champion and Seth (1968) and Negi (1990) into
five main types and sixteen sub-types, on criteria of rainfall, temperature, their seasonal variation
and edaphic and biotic conditions. Tropical Wet Evergreen and Tropical Semi-Evergreen are sub-types 1
and 2 of Group A, Moist Tropical Forests — 5.120 and 2.624 million hectares in Khullar's Table 6.1,
after Sagreiya 1994."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **H.G. Champion (1936)** distinguished **116 types** of vegetation in India.
2. ✅ Simplified by **G.S. Puri (1960)**, **Legris (1963)**, **Champion and S.K. Seth (1968)** and
   **S.S. Negi (1990)** — all five names and dates printed on folio 169.
3. ✅ The result: **5 main types and 16 sub-types**. **"Champion and Seth, 1968" is the standard
   attribution**, not 1936.
4. ✅ The criteria: *"unequal distribution of **rainfall and temperature** as well as their
   **seasonal variation**, besides varied **edaphic and biotic** conditions"*.
5. ✅ **Group A, Moist Tropical:** 1 Tropical Wet Evergreen · 2 Tropical Semi-Evergreen · 3 Tropical
   Moist Deciduous · 4 Littoral and Swamp.
6. ✅ **Group B, Dry Tropical:** 5 Tropical Dry Evergreen · 6 Tropical Dry Deciduous · 7 Tropical
   Thorn.
7. ✅ **Group C, Montane Sub-tropical:** 8 Sub-tropical broad leaved hill · 9 Sub-tropical moist hill
   (**pine**) · 10 Sub-tropical dry evergreen.
8. ✅ **Group D, Montane Temperate:** 11 Montane Wet Temperate · 12 Himalayan Moist Temperate ·
   13 Himalayan Dry Temperate.
9. ✅ **Group E, Alpine:** 14 Sub-Alpine · 15 Moist Alpine Scrub · 16 Dry Alpine Scrub.
10. ⚠️ **The architecture in one line:** groups A and B split on **moisture**; C, D and E are
    successive **thermal-altitudinal** levels.
11. ✅ **Table 6.1 (folio 173, after Sagreiya 1994):** Wet Evergreen **5.120 m ha = 8.0 %**;
    Semi-evergreen **2.624 m ha = 4.1 %**; total **64.000 m ha = 100.0 %**. **Arithmetic re-verified
    at generation.**
12. ⚠️ **India's forest estate is mainly deciduous:** sub-types 3 and 6 = **41.984 m ha = 65.6 %**;
    the two evergreen types = **7.744 m ha = 12.1 %** of the column total — ⚠️ **not of India**.
13. ⚠️ **U4 declared:** **no current official type-wise area table was retrieved**; Table 6.1 is a
    **1994** datum, and **no "FSI forest types" scheme is asserted** (gate **G26**).

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Indian forests are classified by Champion (1936) into 5 types and
16 sub-types." Correct the statement and explain why the correction matters.

**Model answer:** **Champion's 1936 work distinguished 116 types**, not 16. The five-type,
sixteen-sub-type scheme results from successive **simplifications** by **Puri (1960)**, **Legris
(1963)**, **Champion and Seth (1968)** and **Negi (1990)**; the standard attribution is therefore
**Champion and Seth, 1968**. The correction matters because 116 and 16 differ in **resolution, not
in truth** — the later scheme is a usability decision, not a refutation — and because the date fixes
the scheme's **vintage**: it is a 1968 framework, no current official type-wise area table was
retrieved, and Khullar's areas are a **Sagreiya 1994** datum. *(98 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a classification is a discovery about nature. It is an instrument
with authors, a date, stated criteria and thresholds on its edges, and it can be simplified without
anything in the forest changing. *(37 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 13

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Discuss the basis and structure of the Champion–Seth
classification of Indian forests, and state its limitations as an instrument.

**Model:** Khullar records that classification is *"a difficult job"* given India's **unequal
distribution of rainfall and temperature, their seasonal variation, and varied edaphic and biotic
conditions**. **H.G. Champion (1936)** distinguished **116 types**; successive simplification by
**Puri (1960)**, **Legris (1963)**, **Champion and Seth (1968)** and **Negi (1990)** yielded **five
main types and sixteen sub-types**. Its structure is a two-axis grid: Groups **A Moist Tropical** and
**B Dry Tropical** divide on **moisture** at one thermal level, while **C Montane Sub-tropical**,
**D Montane Temperate** and **E Alpine** are successive **thermal-altitudinal** levels. Each class
carries threshold values of rainfall, temperature and humidity. Its limitations are that it is a
**1968 instrument**, that its classes are **conventions with contestable thresholds** — Khullar's own
evergreen threshold differs between consecutive folios — and that the available type-wise areas are a
**1994** datum, no current official table having been retrieved. *(148 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Full lineage with all five authorities and dates **3** · two-axis structure
explained rather than listed **3** · 116-versus-16 resolution point **2** · vintage and
threshold-convention limitations **2**

## Lesson 14 — 250 or 200? Wet evergreen, semi-evergreen, and a book that contradicts itself
