```text
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 14 / 19 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **threshold number line with the two conflicting Khullar ticks**

### The intuition, before any term

This is the lesson where a single number decides a mark.

India's two wettest forest types sit next to each other on a rainfall gradient. Walk westward down
the Western Ghats from the crest toward the coast, or eastward from the crest into the rain shadow,
and you pass from one into the other. The **only** thing that separates them in the classification
is a set of **threshold values** — a rainfall band, a temperature band, a humidity figure.

C2 has merged the two bands into one and attached the merged band to the wrong type. That is the
single most consequential error in the pair, because **the discriminator is exactly what gets
tested**: an examiner who wants to separate candidates on Indian forest types will put 200–250 cm in
one option and over 250 cm in another, and a candidate carrying C2's sentence has no way to choose.

And then there is a second problem, and it is the book's own. **Khullar sets the evergreen threshold
at 200 cm on one printed folio and at 250 cm on the next.** That is not a misprint either, and it is
not something this edition patches. It is **printed, named and left unresolved**, because a silent
repair would teach a false certainty about a boundary the source itself does not hold steady.

**Plain words before the terms.** *Semi-evergreen* does not mean half the trees are evergreen. It
means the stand contains **both evergreen and deciduous elements** in a drier setting, so the canopy
is **less dense and less uniformly leafy** than wet evergreen forest. *Relative humidity* here is a
**mean annual** figure, used as a classification criterion in its own right.

### The primary visual — the threshold number line

```text
   THE INDIAN RAINFALL THRESHOLD LINE — and the conflict inside one book
   All values ✅ printed.  ⇄ marks the unresolved conflict.

   cm/yr   0        50       100      150      200      250      300+
           ├────────┼────────┼────────┼────────┼────────┼────────►
           │        │        │        │        │        │
  FOLIO    │ dry    │ drier  │  monsoon        │ EVERGREEN RAIN  │
   168     │ thorny │ decid. │  deciduous      │ FORESTS         │
  (ladder) │ scrub, │ or     │  forests        │                 │
           │ low    │ trop.  │                 │ ✅ "Areas receiving
           │ open   │ savana │ ✅ "between 100  │    200 cm OR MORE
           │ bush → │ → open │    and 200 cm"  │    … have stands of
           │ semi   │ thorny │                 │    EVERGREEN RAIN
           │ desert │ scrub  │                 │    FORESTS"
           │        │        │        │    ▲   │        │
           │        │        │        │    ⇄ CONFLICT   │
           │        │        │        │    ▼   │        │
  FOLIO    │        │        │        │ ╔══════╧═══╗ ╔══╧═══════════╗
   169     │        │        │        │ ║ 2. TROP. ║ ║ 1. TROPICAL  ║
  (classes)│        │        │        │ ║ SEMI-    ║ ║ WET EVERGREEN║
           │        │        │        │ ║ EVERGRN  ║ ║              ║
           │        │        │        │ ║ 200–250  ║ ║ EXCEEDS 250  ║
           │        │        │        │ ║ cm       ║ ║ cm           ║
           │        │        │        │ ╚══════════╝ ╚══════════════╝
           │        │        │        │      ▲              ▲
           │        │        │        │      │              │
           │        │        │        │  ✗ C2 lines 28 & 34 take THIS
           │        │        │        │    band and label it THAT type:
           │        │        │        │    "wet evergreen … exceeds about
           │        │        │        │     200–250 cm"
           │        │        │        │
   ╔═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
   ║  THE SAME BOOK, ONE PRINTED FOLIO APART:                          ║
   ║    folio 168 →  evergreen rain forest begins at 200 cm            ║
   ║    folio 169 →  Tropical Wet Evergreen requires MORE THAN 250 cm  ║
   ║  ⇄ THIS CONFLICT IS PRINTED, NAMED, AND LEFT **UNRESOLVED**.      ║
   ╚═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

   AND THE DISCRIMINATOR IS NOT RAINFALL ALONE — THREE VARIABLES:
   ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
   │                  │ 1. WET EVERGREEN │ 2. SEMI-EVERGREEN           │
   ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
   │ annual rainfall  │ EXCEEDS 250 cm   │ 200–250 cm                  │
   │ annual temp.     │ about 25–27 °C   │ varies from 24 °C to 27 °C  │
   │ mean annual RH   │ EXCEEDS 77 %     │ about 75 %                  │
   │ dry season       │ DISTINCTLY SHORT │ "comparatively drier areas" │
   │ density          │ "lofty, VERY     │ "LESS DENSE but MORE        │
   │                  │  dense, multi-   │  GREGARIOUS (especially in  │
   │                  │  layered"        │  Assam) than the wet"       │
   └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘
   ► Note the OVERLAP in temperature (25–27 inside 24–27) and the
     SMALL GAP in humidity (77 vs 75).  RAINFALL is the sharp edge;
     the other two are supporting, not independent, discriminators.
```

**Caption.** Two adjacent classes, three criteria, one sharp edge — and a book that puts the edge in
two different places on consecutive pages.

### ✅ The two entries, printed in full

**✅ Khullar folio 169 (scan PDF 101 right), entry 1, read directly at generation:**

> "**1. Tropical Wet Evergreen Forests.** These are **typical rain forests** which grow in those
> areas where the **annual rainfall exceeds 250 cm**, the **annual temperature is about 25°-27°C**,
> the **average annual humidity exceeds 77 per cent** and the **dry season is distinctly short**.
> Due to high heat and high humidity, the trees of these forests **do not shed their leaves
> annually, at least not together**, and are termed as **evergreen forests**. These are **lofty,
> very dense multilayered forests with mesophytic evergreens**. The trees often reach **45 metres**
> in height, **individual trees exceed 60 metres**. The entire morphology looks like a **green
> carpet when viewed from above**. The **sun light cannot reach the ground** and owing to deep
> shade, the **undergrowth is formed mainly of tangled mass of canes, bamboos, ferns, climbers**,
> etc. A **variety of orchids flourishes on the trees**."

**✅ Khullar folio 169, entry 2, read directly at generation:**

> "**2. Tropical Semi-Evergreen Forests.** **Bordering the areas of tropical wet evergreen forests**,
> are **comparatively drier areas** of the tropical semi-evergreen forests. Here the **annual
> rainfall is 200-250 cm**, the **mean annual temperature varies from 24°C to 27°C** and the
> **relative humidity is about 75 per cent**. Obviously these forests are found on the **Western
> coast, Assam, lower slopes of the Eastern Himalayas, Odisha and Andamans**. The semi-evergreen
> forests are **less dense but more gregarious (especially in Assam) than the wet** [evergreen
> forests]."

### ✗ Correction C11 — the band belongs to the other type

| | ✗ **C2 lines 28 and 34** | ✅ **Khullar folio 169** |
|---|---|---|
| Tropical **Wet** Evergreen | *"rainfall exceeds about **200–250 cm**"* | *"the annual rainfall **exceeds 250 cm**"* |
| Tropical **Semi**-Evergreen | not separately specified | *"the annual rainfall is **200-250 cm**"* |
| Temperature signature | **absent** | Wet **25–27 °C** · Semi **24–27 °C** |
| Humidity signature | **absent** | Wet **exceeds 77 %** · Semi **about 75 %** |
| Dry season | *"the dry season is short"* | Wet *"**distinctly short**"* · Semi *"comparatively drier areas"* |

⚠️ **Why this is the highest-severity correction in the pair, stated precisely.** The error is not a
loose figure. It takes **the whole of the semi-evergreen band** and attaches it to **wet evergreen**,
with the result that the two classes **cannot be told apart at all** by a reader using C2. And the
phrasing *"exceeds about 200–250 cm"* is additionally incoherent as a criterion — a threshold
cannot be a band, and "exceeds a band" has no determinate meaning. Replace it with the two printed
entries and the discrimination is restored in one table.

⚠️ **Note what C2's sentence does to the ladder as well.** C2's band spans the folio-168 threshold
(200 cm) and the folio-169 threshold (250 cm), so it **silently absorbs the book's own conflict**
instead of reporting it. One error has concealed another.

### ⇄ Correction C12 — Khullar against Khullar, and the refusal to patch it

**✅ Folio 168 (scan PDF 101 left), *Factors Influencing Vegetation*:**

> "Areas receiving **200 cm or more** rainfall per annum have stands of **evergreen rain forests**,
> while monsoon deciduous forests dominate in areas with rainfall between 100 and 200 cm."

**✅ Folio 169 (scan PDF 101 right), *Classification of Natural Vegetation*, entry 1:**

> "…areas where the **annual rainfall exceeds 250 cm**…"

**The same author, the same chapter, consecutive printed folios, two different thresholds for the
beginning of evergreen rain forest.** ⇄ **This conflict is named here and left UNRESOLVED.**

⚠️ **Why it is left unresolved rather than reconciled, and why that is the stronger teaching.** A
reconciliation is available and is **tempting**: folio 168 is a **broad vegetation generalisation**
covering *"evergreen rain forests"* as a loose category, which would naturally include both wet
evergreen **and** semi-evergreen, whose combined range begins at **200 cm**; folio 169 is a
**formal class definition** for one sub-type, requiring **over 250 cm**. On that reading the two
pages are compatible.

**But the book does not say this.** It does not cross-refer, it does not qualify folio 168, and it
does not state that *"evergreen rain forests"* on folio 168 is a broader category than *"Tropical
Wet Evergreen"* on folio 169. **The reconciliation is the reader's inference, not the author's
statement**, and presenting it as settled would be the same category of error this edition exists
to correct — supplying an inference under a mark meaning *from the source book*.

> **The frozen handling.** Print **both folios side by side**. Name the conflict. Use the word
> **unresolved**. Offer the possible reconciliation explicitly as ⚠️ **reader's inference**, clearly
> separated. **Do not adopt it.** The defensible examination sentence is: *"Khullar sets the
> evergreen threshold at 200 cm at folio 168 and requires over 250 cm for Tropical Wet Evergreen at
> folio 169; the two are not reconciled in the text."*

⚠️ **And a practical note that makes the refusal useful rather than merely scrupulous.** If an option
says *"Tropical Wet Evergreen forests occur where rainfall exceeds 250 cm"*, it matches **folio 169**
— the **formal class definition** — and is the safer reading of a **forest-type** question. If an
option says *"evergreen rain forests occur above 200 cm"*, it matches **folio 168** and is defensible
for a **general vegetation** question. **Knowing which page a statement comes from is the skill; it
is not the same as knowing which page is right.**

### ✅ The other printed discriminators, which C2 omits entirely

**Temperature.** Wet evergreen **about 25–27 °C**; semi-evergreen **24 °C to 27 °C**. ⚠️ The wet
range sits **inside** the semi range, so temperature **cannot discriminate on its own** — it is a
supporting criterion. Saying so is itself a mark, because it shows you have read the two entries
against each other rather than memorised them separately.

**Humidity.** Wet evergreen *"**exceeds 77 per cent**"*; semi-evergreen *"**about 75 per cent**"*.
⚠️ A gap of about two percentage points — small, but **printed, directional and consistent with the
rainfall ordering**. ⚠️ And set it beside the world-side figure from **Lesson 5**: Leong gives the
equatorial type *"constantly high (**over 80 per cent**)"*. **Three printed humidity figures in one
topic: over 80 % (world type), above 77 % (Indian wet evergreen), about 75 % (Indian
semi-evergreen).** They descend in exactly the order the moisture gradient demands, and they are
made for close options.

**Structure and density.** Wet evergreen is *"**lofty, very dense multilayered** … with
**mesophytic evergreens**"*, a *"**green carpet** when viewed from above"*, where *"the **sun light
cannot reach the ground**"*. Semi-evergreen is *"**less dense but more gregarious** (especially in
**Assam**) than the wet"*.

⚠️ **"More gregarious" is the most useful word in the semi-evergreen entry and it is almost always
skipped.** *Gregarious* means **individuals of one species growing together** — in forestry, the
tendency to form **purer stands**. That is a direct reversal of the equatorial *"absence of pure
stands"* obstacle from **Lesson 10**. So semi-evergreen forest, being less diverse and more
gregarious, is **more exploitable** than wet evergreen forest. ⚠️ **One adjective carries the whole
extraction economics of the type**, and Khullar locates the effect precisely: *especially in Assam*.

**The undergrowth, and a contrast worth holding.** Khullar's wet evergreen undergrowth is a
*"**tangled mass of canes, bamboos, ferns, climbers**"* because *"the sun light cannot reach the
ground"*. ⚠️ Compare **Leong printed p. 118** on the selva: *"**Because the trees cut out most of
the sunlight the undergrowth is not dense.**"* **The two books give opposite answers from the same
premise.** Both attribute the state of the floor to deep shade; one concludes sparse, the other
tangled. ⇄ This edition **records the divergence and does not resolve it**, noting only that Indian
stands are typically **steeper, more fragmented and more disturbed**, which favours denser
undergrowth — ⚠️ an inference, not either book's statement.

### Causal chain, assumptions and limits

```text
  rainfall amount DECLINES along a gradient (down-slope, leeward, poleward)
        │
        ▼
  >250 cm, RH >77 %, dry season "distinctly short"
        │
        ├──► no synchronised leaf fall  ──► ✅ "do not shed their leaves
        │    annually, AT LEAST NOT TOGETHER"
        │         │
        │         ▼
        │    ✅ TROPICAL WET EVERGREEN: lofty, very dense, multilayered,
        │       45 m commonly / >60 m individually, green carpet,
        │       sunlight cannot reach the ground, tangled undergrowth
        │         │
        │         └──► ⚠️ high diversity, low conspecific density
        │              ──► ✅ Khullar folio 169: "NOT PROPERLY EXPLOITED"
        ▼
  200–250 cm, RH ~75 %, "comparatively drier"
        │
        ├──► SOME species find the dry spell long enough to shed
        │         │
        │         ▼
        │    ✅ TROPICAL SEMI-EVERGREEN: "LESS DENSE but MORE GREGARIOUS"
        │         │
        │         └──► ⚠️ MORE GREGARIOUS = purer stands = MORE EXPLOITABLE
        │              ──► a direct reversal of the "no pure stands" obstacle
        ▼
  100–200 cm
        └──► ✅ folio 168: MONSOON DECIDUOUS FORESTS  ↪ Geography/basic/17
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ⇄ AND THE UNRESOLVED EDGE:
     folio 168 puts "evergreen rain forests" at 200 cm
     folio 169 puts "Tropical Wet Evergreen" above 250 cm
     ⚠️ possible reconciliation (broad category vs formal class) is the
        READER'S INFERENCE and is NOT ADOPTED HERE.
```

**Assumptions.** That mean annual values can classify a stand; that the gradient is monotonic; that
the thresholds measured in the mid-twentieth century still describe current rainfall regimes.

**What this chain does NOT explain.** It does not explain **where on the ground the line falls**,
because the transition is gradational. It does not explain **why Khullar's two folios differ** — the
text gives no reason. And it does not address the **Köppen Am/Aw distinction**, which is a different
seasonality threshold on a different scheme and is ↪ `Geography/basic/14`.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison — four threshold statements in this topic, and what each governs.**

| Statement | Source | Governs |
|---|---|---|
| **60–100 inches (152–254 cm)** | ✅ Leong p. 117 | The **world type** |
| **about 200 cm** | ✅ Majid folio 5.3 | The **ETR biome** globally |
| **200 cm or more** | ✅ Khullar folio 168 | **Indian "evergreen rain forests"**, broad |
| **exceeds 250 cm** | ✅ Khullar folio 169 | **Indian Tropical Wet Evergreen**, formal class |

⚠️ Four numbers, four **objects**. Only the last two are in conflict, because only they are about the
same country and the same vegetation category.

**Objection.** "The reconciliation is obvious — folio 168 is loose, folio 169 is precise. Teaching an
'unresolved conflict' is manufactured difficulty."

**Reply.** The reconciliation is **plausible and is stated above as such**, so nothing is being
hidden. But three things stop it being adopted. **First**, it is **not in the book**: folio 168 does
not say its category is broader, and the author never cross-refers. Adopting a reader's inference
under a book mark is precisely the defect this edition was built to correct — it is what produced
C1's six strata and C1's rosewood. **Second**, the proposed reading requires folio 168's *"evergreen
rain forests"* to include **semi-evergreen** forest, which is awkward, since semi-evergreen is by
definition **not** fully evergreen and Khullar classes it separately. **Third and practically**, an
examiner can set a statement matching **either** folio, and a candidate who has been taught "250 cm,
settled" will mark a true folio-168 statement false. **Knowing that the source carries both numbers
is strictly more useful than believing it carries one.**

**Unresolved residual.** ⇄ **Khullar's evergreen rainfall threshold is 200 cm at folio 168 and over
250 cm at folio 169, and the text does not reconcile them. This edition leaves the conflict
unresolved.** What it fixes is C2's error, which is a **different** matter: the **200–250 cm band
belongs to semi-evergreen**, and that is printed without ambiguity.

### India hook — a Western Ghats transect

🇮🇳 **The hook.** Walk a transect across the Western Ghats at, say, the latitude of the Nilgiri
Hills. On the **windward crest and upper western slopes**, rainfall is at its maximum, the dry
season is at its shortest, and the forest is **Tropical Wet Evergreen** — lofty, closed, multilayered,
a *"green carpet"* from above. Move **down and away** from the crest and rainfall falls through the
250 cm line into the 200–250 cm band; the stand thins, deciduous elements appear, and it becomes
**Tropical Semi-Evergreen** — *"less dense but more gregarious"*. Continue and you drop below 200 cm
into **monsoon deciduous** forest. ✅ Khullar's own semi-evergreen distribution — *"Western coast,
Assam, lower slopes of the Eastern Himalayas, Odisha and Andamans"* — is exactly this
**bordering** position: ✅ *"**Bordering the areas of tropical wet evergreen forests**, are
comparatively drier areas"*.

**The stated limitation.** **The transition is gradational; the threshold is a convention, not a
line.** There is no point on the slope where the forest changes. The classes are **classes**, with
all the properties Lesson 13 established — authors, a date, criteria, and thresholds whose values
the author himself recorded differently on consecutive folios. ⚠️ On the ground there is a **mosaic
and an ecotone**, with aspect, soil depth, past felling and fire all shifting the boundary locally.
What the transect teaches is the **ordering** of the classes, which is robust; what it cannot teach
is where the line is, which is not.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Attaching 200–250 cm to Tropical Wet Evergreen.** That is the **semi-evergreen** band. Wet
  evergreen *"**exceeds 250 cm**"*. **This is the single most testable error in the pair** (gate
  **G9**).
- ❌ **Writing "exceeds about 200–250 cm".** A threshold cannot be a band; the formulation has no
  determinate meaning.
- ❌ **Silently resolving the folio 168 / folio 169 conflict.** ⇄ **Print both, name it, use the word
  unresolved** (gate **G10**).
- ❌ **Omitting the humidity figures.** **Above 77 %** against **about 75 %** is a printed,
  directional discriminator, and both are absent from C2.
- ❌ **Treating temperature as a discriminator.** **25–27 °C sits inside 24–27 °C**; it cannot
  separate the two types alone.
- ❌ **Skipping "more gregarious".** It means **purer stands**, and therefore **greater
  exploitability** — the economic consequence of the classification.
- ❌ **Assuming semi-evergreen means half the trees are evergreen.** It is a **drier, mixed,
  less dense** formation bordering the wet evergreen.
- ❌ **Confusing these thresholds with Köppen Am and Aw.** Different scheme, different criterion
  (seasonality, not amount). ↪ `Geography/basic/14`.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"distribution of key natural resources across … the Indian subcontinent"* —
the two wettest Indian forest sub-types, their defining criteria and their boundary.

**PYQ position.** **Topic 15 owns no routed PYQ for this content, and C2 owns none anywhere in
2018–2026** (`PYQ-INTEGRATION-AUDIT-2018-2023.md` line 636; `...2024-2025.md` line 352;
`...2026.md` line 420). This lesson nevertheless carries the **highest Prelims risk** in the
edition, because threshold discrimination between adjacent forest types is exactly the kind of
statement-level demand the ledgers record for vegetation topics. Topic 15's own demands stay at
`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` lines **453** and **666**, both `Routed; key unavailable
locally`. **No answer letter is inferred, implied or reconstructed anywhere in this edition.**

**Answer use.** Give **three criteria, two classes, and the conflict** in four sentences.
*"Khullar's Tropical Wet Evergreen requires annual rainfall exceeding 250 cm, temperature about
25–27 °C, mean annual humidity above 77 per cent and a distinctly short dry season; Tropical
Semi-Evergreen borders it in comparatively drier areas with 200–250 cm, 24–27 °C and about 75 per
cent humidity, and is 'less dense but more gregarious'. Note that Khullar's folio 168 sets the
evergreen threshold at 200 cm while folio 169 requires over 250 cm; the two are not reconciled in
the text. The transition on the ground is gradational, so the threshold is a classificatory
convention."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Tropical Wet Evergreen:** rainfall **exceeds 250 cm** · temperature **about 25–27 °C** ·
   mean annual humidity **exceeds 77 %** · dry season **distinctly short**.
2. ✅ **Tropical Semi-Evergreen:** rainfall **200–250 cm** · temperature **24–27 °C** · relative
   humidity **about 75 %** · *"comparatively drier areas"*.
3. ✗ **C11, the highest-severity correction:** C2 lines 28 and 34 attach *"exceeds about
   200–250 cm"* to **wet** evergreen. **That band is semi-evergreen's.**
4. ⇄ **C12, unresolved:** folio **168** — *"**200 cm or more** … evergreen rain forests"*; folio
   **169** — *"exceeds **250 cm**"*. **Printed one folio apart; not reconciled in the text; left
   unresolved here.**
5. ⚠️ The available reconciliation — broad category versus formal class — is **the reader's
   inference** and is **not adopted**.
6. ⚠️ **Practical rule:** a *"forest-type"* statement matches **folio 169**; a *"general vegetation"*
   statement matches **folio 168**. Know which page a statement comes from.
7. ✅ **Evergreen defined:** trees *"**do not shed their leaves annually, at least not together**"*.
8. ✅ Wet evergreen structure: *"**lofty, very dense multilayered** … **mesophytic evergreens**"*;
   **45 m** commonly, **individuals over 60 m**; *"**green carpet** when viewed from above"*; *"the
   **sun light cannot reach the ground**"*; undergrowth a *"**tangled mass of canes, bamboos, ferns,
   climbers**"*; *"a variety of **orchids flourishes on the trees**"*.
9. ⇄ **Undergrowth divergence:** Khullar's Indian wet evergreen floor is **tangled**; ✅ Leong's
   selva floor *"is **not dense**"* — **same premise, opposite conclusion**, recorded and not
   resolved.
10. ✅ **Semi-evergreen is *"less dense but more gregarious (especially in Assam) than the wet"*.**
    ⚠️ **Gregarious = purer stands = more exploitable** — the reversal of the Lesson 10 obstacle.
11. ✅ Semi-evergreen distribution: **Western coast, Assam, lower slopes of the Eastern Himalayas,
    Odisha and Andamans**; it **borders** the wet evergreen areas.
12. ⚠️ **Temperature cannot discriminate:** **25–27 °C** lies **inside** **24–27 °C**.
13. ⚠️ **Three printed humidity figures, descending with moisture:** **over 80 %** (Leong, world
    type) · **above 77 %** (Khullar, Indian wet evergreen) · **about 75 %** (Khullar, Indian
    semi-evergreen).
14. ✅ **The ladder below:** **100–200 cm** monsoon deciduous (folio 168) ↪ `Geography/basic/17`.
15. ⚠️ The transition is **gradational**; the threshold is a **convention**, and the classes are
    1968 classes (Lesson 13).

### Concept check

**Question:** Your notes state that tropical wet evergreen forests occur where rainfall "exceeds
about 200–250 cm". Correct this, and state what Khullar's own two folios say about the threshold.

**Model answer:** The band is **semi-evergreen's**. Khullar folio 169 gives **Tropical Wet
Evergreen** rainfall *"exceeds 250 cm"*, temperature **25–27 °C**, humidity **above 77 %** and a
*"distinctly short"* dry season; **Tropical Semi-Evergreen** takes **200–250 cm**, **24–27 °C** and
about **75 %**, in *"comparatively drier areas"* bordering the wet evergreen. The notes have merged
two classes and destroyed the discriminator. Separately, **Khullar contradicts himself**: folio 168
says *"200 cm or more … evergreen rain forests"* while folio 169 requires over **250 cm**. The two
are **not reconciled in the text**, and the conflict should be reported, not patched. *(102 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a threshold quoted in a textbook is a single settled value. This
author records two, one folio apart, so a safe response states both and their locations rather than
choosing. *(34 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 14

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Distinguish India's tropical wet evergreen from its
tropical semi-evergreen forests, and comment on the reliability of the threshold that separates them.

**Model:** Khullar separates them on **three** criteria. **Tropical Wet Evergreen** requires rainfall
*"exceeds 250 cm"*, temperature *"about 25°-27°C"*, humidity *"exceeds 77 per cent"* and a dry
season *"distinctly short"*; it is *"lofty, very dense multilayered"*, reaching **45 m** commonly and
**over 60 m** individually, a *"green carpet when viewed from above"* with a tangled understorey of
canes, bamboos, ferns and climbers. **Tropical Semi-Evergreen** borders it in *"comparatively drier
areas"* with **200–250 cm**, **24–27 °C** and about **75 per cent** humidity, and is *"less dense but
more gregarious (especially in Assam)"* — hence more exploitable. **Reliability is limited.** The
temperature ranges overlap, so only rainfall is a sharp edge; and the same author sets the evergreen
threshold at **200 cm** on folio 168 and above **250 cm** on folio 169 without reconciling them. The
transition is in any case gradational. *(150 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** All three criteria correctly assigned to the correct type **4** · structural
and gregariousness contrast with its economic consequence **2** · the intra-source threshold conflict
reported and left unresolved **3** · overlap and gradational caveat **1**

| Tropical Moist Deciduous and monsoon deciduous forests — the next class down | `Geography/basic/17_Savanna-Sudan-Climate`, `Geography/advanced/17` |

```text
                   Ten Degree Channel, Great Channel, Indira Point.
                   ABSENT for ANAIMALAI HILLS as an entity, AGASTHYAMALAI, MALABAR
                   COAST, AGUMBE — all located by description only (H18, gate G25)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 15 / 19 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **three-belt relief sketch with altitude bands marked**

### The intuition, before any term

India's evergreen forests are not scattered. They sit in **three discrete belts**, and each belt is
in its place for the **same reason**: it is the first high ground that a moisture-laden flow meets.

The Western Ghats intercept the south-west monsoon as it comes off the Arabian Sea. The North-Eastern
hills intercept the Bay of Bengal branch as it is funnelled up the Brahmaputra lowland. The Andaman
and Nicobar Islands sit in the open Bay of Bengal and are wet because they are **surrounded by
warm ocean** in the path of the flow.

So the distribution map is really a **mechanism map**: it shows you where Arrow 3 of Lesson 4 —
orographic rain — operates hardest in India, plus one insular case where it is not needed. And the
corollary is immediate and examinable: these belts are **narrow, linear and one-sided**. Cross the
crest and you are in rain shadow within a few tens of kilometres. ✅ C2 line 37 states this
correctly: *"Windward slope evergreen; leeward side drier."*

**Plain words before the terms.** *Windward* is the side a wind arrives from; *leeward* is the
sheltered side. An *altitude band* means the forest type occupies a **range of heights**, with
different types above and below it — so the belt is a **ribbon on a slope**, not a blanket over a
region.

**Caption.** Three belts, two printed altitude bands, six North-Eastern States in printed order, two
species sets sharing exactly two names. Odisha belongs to the semi-evergreen list only.

### ✅ The distribution sentence, printed

**✅ Khullar folio 169 (scan PDF 101 right)**, read directly at generation:

> "The **true evergreen forests** are found along the **western side of the Western Ghats (between
> 500 to 1370 metres above sea level) south of Mumbai**, in a **strip running from north-east to
> south-west direction across Arunachal Pradesh, Upper Assam, Nagaland, Manipur, Mizoram and Tripura
> upto a height of 1070 metres** and in the **Andaman and Nicobar Islands**."

**Four examinable precisions in one sentence, every one of them absent from C2.**

| ✅ Printed precision | Why it is examinable |
|---|---|
| **"western side"** of the Western Ghats | The **windward** side. The eastern side is rain-shadowed — ✅ C2 line 37 and C2 line 70 both get this right |
| **"between 500 to 1370 metres"** | A **lower** bound as well as an upper one. The type is **not** at sea level and **not** at the summits |
| **"south of Mumbai"** | A **latitudinal** cut-off on the belt. The northern Ghats are drier and more seasonal |
| **"upto a height of 1070 metres"** in the North-East | A **different, lower** ceiling than the Ghats — two belts, two altitude limits |

⚠️ **The lower bound of 500 m is the one that surprises people and the one most worth holding.** Why
should wet evergreen forest **not** occur on the coastal lowland immediately below, where rainfall is
also very high? Two reasons, both standard: the **lowland is where people are**, so it has been
cleared for settlement and plantation for centuries; and the **orographic enhancement** that drives
the rainfall maximum occurs **on the slope**, not at its foot. ⚠️ Khullar does not give a reason — the
bound is printed without explanation, and this edition marks the explanation as inference.

⚠️ **And the two different ceilings are a mechanism point, not a coincidence.** **1,370 m** in the
Ghats against **1,070 m** in the North-East. The ceiling is set by **temperature falling with
altitude** until the tropical class gives way to a sub-tropical or montane one — and the North-East
lies further **north**, so the same thermal limit is reached at a **lower** elevation. ↪ What lies
above those ceilings — Groups C and D of the Champion–Seth scheme — is `Geography/advanced/22`.

### ✅ The two species sets, and the two names they share

**✅ Khullar folio 169, verbatim:**

> "The timber of these forests is **fine-grained, hard and durable** as a result of which it has
> **high commercial value**. The important species of these forests are **mesua, white cedar,
> calophyllum, toon, dhup, palaquium, hopea, jamun, canes**, etc. in the **Western Ghats**, and
> **gurjan, chaplasha, jamun, mesua, agar, muli, bamboo** etc. in the **Himalayan region**."

| | **Western Ghats** | **Himalayan / North-East** |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Species | **mesua · white cedar · calophyllum · toon · dhup · palaquium · hopea · jamun · canes** | **gurjan · chaplasha · jamun · mesua · agar · muli · bamboo** |
| Shared | **jamun**, **mesua** | **jamun**, **mesua** |
| Distinctive | white cedar, calophyllum, toon, dhup, palaquium, **hopea** | **gurjan**, **chaplasha**, **agar**, **muli** |

⚠️ **Three observations that turn a list into an argument.**

1. **Exactly two names are shared.** **Jamun** and **mesua** occur in both. Everything else is
   **region-specific**. Two belts of the same forest type, two thousand kilometres apart, with
   largely different floras — which is what you expect if the type is defined by **climate and
   structure** rather than by species composition.
2. **Not one equatorial genus appears.** No mahogany, no ebony, no greenheart, no rosewood (**Lesson
   7**). ✅ C2 line 36 notes that *"mahogany is largely introduced"* in India. The architecture
   transfers; the flora does not.
3. **Canes and bamboo appear in both lists**, as they did in the equatorial life-form matrix. ⚠️
   **Canes are the Indian rattans** — the same life-form that supplied the Orang Asli cane economy
   of **Lesson 9**. The **life-form spectrum** is the thing that travels.

⚠️ **And the commercial sentence matters for the next paragraph.** Khullar explicitly says the timber
is *"fine-grained, hard and durable"* with *"high commercial value"*. The problem with these forests
is **not** that the timber is poor.

### ✅ Why the belts are not exploited — the Indian parallel to Leong and Majid

**✅ Khullar folio 169, immediately following:**

> "Although these forests contain **valuable commercial species**, they have **not been properly
> exploited** due to **dense undergrowth**, **absence of pure stands** and **lack of transport
> facilities**."

| Khullar's Indian reason | Equatorial counterpart | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **absence of pure stands** | *"trees **do not occur in homogenous stands**"* · *"once or twice per km²"* | ✅ Leong p. 120 · ✅ Majid 5.4 |
| **dense undergrowth** | ⇄ **the reverse** of Leong's *"the undergrowth is not dense"* — the divergence recorded at Lesson 14 | ✅ Leong p. 118 |
| **lack of transport facilities** | ⚠️ the nearest counterpart is *"no frozen surfaces to facilitate logging"* and *"too heavy to float"* — **different constraints, same consequence** | ✅ Leong p. 120 |

★ **"Absence of pure stands" is printed in all three books — Leong p. 120, Majid folio 5.4 and
Khullar folio 169.** That three-source convergence is the strongest evidence in this edition for the
proposition that the Indian wet evergreen forest faces the **same extraction economics** as the
equatorial selva despite having an entirely different flora. It is the right sentence to deploy in
any comparative answer.

**✅ And the national frame, from Khullar folio 174 (scan PDF 103 left)**, read directly at
generation: about **half** of India's forests are **exploitable**, about **one fourth** *"potentially
exploitable"*; exploitable and potentially exploitable forests occur in **Assam, Arunachal Pradesh,
Tripura, Western Ghats, Satpura, Maikal, Chota Nagpur plateau, Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Odisha
and adjoining areas of Andhra Pradesh and Chhattisgarh**; and *"a large proportion of our forests is
**inaccessible** for effective exploitation and are also termed as **non-merchantable**"*, lying *"in
the high reaches of the Himalayas in Kashmir and Arunachal Pradesh where they cannot be exploited due
to **lack of transport facilities**"*.

⚠️ **This is the passage that produced C1's error.** As **Lesson 10** established, *"inaccessible"*
is a printed **Indian** forestry term, attached to **high Himalayan** forests, in **Khullar** — and it
migrated into a Leong-framed **equatorial** section in C1. Here it is in its correct place and with
its correct referent.

### Causal chain, assumptions and limits

```text
  SW monsoon flow off the Arabian Sea
        │
        ▼
  meets the WESTERN SIDE of a steep continuous barrier south of Mumbai
        │
        ├──► orographic enhancement maximal ON THE SLOPE, not at its foot
        │         └──► ✅ lower bound 500 m
        │
        ├──► rainfall > 250 cm, dry season distinctly short
        │         └──► ✅ TROPICAL WET EVERGREEN, 500–1370 m
        │
        ├──► above 1370 m: temperature falls below the tropical class
        │         └──► ↪ montane sub-tropical / temperate, Geography/advanced/22
        │
        └──► across the crest: RAIN SHADOW
                  └──► ✅ C2 line 37: "leeward side drier" → semi-evergreen →
                       monsoon deciduous → drier types (the folio 168 ladder)

  Bay of Bengal branch, funnelled up the Brahmaputra lowland
        │
        ▼
  meets the NE hills: strip across ARUNACHAL PRADESH · UPPER ASSAM ·
  NAGALAND · MANIPUR · MIZORAM · TRIPURA
        └──► ceiling 1070 m — LOWER than the Ghats because the belt lies
             FURTHER NORTH, so the thermal limit arrives sooner  ⚠️ inference

  ANDAMAN AND NICOBAR: insular, surrounded by warm sea in the monsoon path
        └──► ✅ no altitude band printed — the mechanism is MARITIME, not
             orographic
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  CONSEQUENCE FOR USE, ✅ folio 169:
  valuable, fine-grained, hard, durable timber
        ├──► ✗ dense undergrowth
        ├──► ✗ ABSENCE OF PURE STANDS  ★ printed in ALL THREE BOOKS
        └──► ✗ lack of transport facilities
                  └──► "NOT PROPERLY EXPLOITED"
```

**Assumptions.** That the barrier is continuous and steep enough; that the flow is not diverted; that
present distribution still reflects climatic potential rather than clearance history.

**What this chain does NOT explain.** It does not explain **the Palghat Gap** or other breaks in the
Ghats, which are structural features routed to the Indian physiography owners. It does not explain
**island formation, tectonics or the Great Nicobar project** — ↪ `Geography/advanced/11`. And it
does not supply **state-wise forest-cover rankings**, which are ↪ `Env-Ecology/basic/11` and own
**2019 Prelims Q33**.

### India hook — the Anaimalai and Agasthyamalai evergreen blocks

🇮🇳 **The hook.** The southern Western Ghats carry the most extensive and least broken wet evergreen
tracts in peninsular India, in the **Anaimalai Hills** and the **Agasthyamalai** range south of the
**Palghat Gap**. They sit squarely inside Khullar's printed band — **western side, 500 to 1,370
metres, south of Mumbai** — and they carry the Western Ghats species set: **mesua, hopea,
calophyllum, palaquium, dhup**. They are the natural Indian terminus of the Amazon-to-India
comparison that closes this edition.

**The stated limitation — and it is a disclosure, not a hedge.** ⚠️ **Map-bank rows are absent for
Anaimalai Hills as an entity and for Agasthyamalai**, and absent also for **Malabar Coast** and
**Agumbe**. `upsc-ai-kit\knowledge\GEOGRAPHY-MAP-LOCATION-BANK.md` **does** carry Western Ghats,
Nilgiri Hills, Palghat Gap, Silent Valley NP, Periyar NP and Eravikulam NP, and the Anaimalais are
named only **inside** two other rows rather than having one of their own. **These locations are
therefore taught from text description only, and this edition does not imply that a canonical map-bank
row exists.** The map bank is a **prohibited write** for this lane, so the gap is **declared and left
open** (**H18**, gate **G25**). In practice: use **Silent Valley**, **Periyar** or **Eravikulam** when
a located example is needed, and describe the Anaimalai and Agasthyamalai blocks by their position
relative to the **Palghat Gap**, which does have a row.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Omitting the altitude bands.** ✅ **500–1,370 m** in the Western Ghats; **up to 1,070 m** in
  the North-East. Both printed, both absent from C2.
- ❌ **Forgetting the lower bound.** The type is **not** at sea level; it begins at **500 m** in the
  Ghats.
- ❌ **Dropping "south of Mumbai".** It is a printed **latitudinal** cut-off on the belt.
- ❌ **Putting evergreen forest on the eastern Ghats slope.** ✅ *"**western side**"*; ✅ C2 line 37:
  *"leeward side drier"*.
- ❌ **Listing the North-Eastern States loosely.** ✅ Printed and in printed order: **Arunachal
  Pradesh, Upper Assam, Nagaland, Manipur, Mizoram, Tripura**.
- ❌ **Putting Odisha in the wet evergreen list.** ✅ Odisha appears in the **semi-evergreen**
  distribution only.
- ❌ **Importing equatorial genera.** ✗ Not one shared; ✅ C2: mahogany is *"largely introduced"*.
- ❌ **Treating the two species sets as interchangeable.** Only **jamun** and **mesua** are common.
- ❌ **Implying a map-bank row exists for the Anaimalais or Agasthyamalai.** ⚠️ It does not; **gap
  declared, not closed**.
- ❌ **Answering a state-wise forest-cover ranking question.** ↪ **2019 Prelims Q33** is routed to
  `Env-Ecology/basic/11`.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"distribution of key natural resources across … the Indian subcontinent"* —
the spatial distribution of India's wet evergreen and semi-evergreen forest resource.

**PYQ position.** **Topic 15 owns no routed PYQ for this content, and C2 owns none in 2018–2026**
(`PYQ-INTEGRATION-AUDIT-2018-2023.md` line 636; `...2024-2025.md` line 352; `...2026.md` line 420).
**This lesson supports `Env-Ecology/basic/11`** (state-wise forest-cover rankings, **2019 Prelims
Q33**) and **`Geography/advanced/11`** (island geography), **neither of which it answers**. Topic
15's own two demands remain at `_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` lines **453** and **666**, both
`Routed; key unavailable locally`. **No answer letter is inferred anywhere.**

**Answer use.** Give the belts **with their altitude bands and their mechanism**, then the use
constraint. *"India's tropical wet evergreen forests occupy three belts: the western side of the
Western Ghats between 500 and 1,370 metres south of Mumbai; a north-east to south-west strip across
Arunachal Pradesh, Upper Assam, Nagaland, Manipur, Mizoram and Tripura up to 1,070 metres; and the
Andaman and Nicobar Islands. The first two are orographic, the third maritime; the lower Ghats bound
reflects clearance and the position of the orographic maximum, and the lower North-Eastern ceiling
reflects latitude. Species differ — mesua, hopea, calophyllum, dhup in the Ghats; gurjan, chaplasha,
agar, muli in the North-East — and despite valuable timber the forests are 'not properly exploited'
owing to dense undergrowth, absence of pure stands and lack of transport."*

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Belt 1:** *"along the **western side** of the Western Ghats (between **500 to 1370 metres**
   above sea level) **south of Mumbai**"*.
2. ✅ **Belt 2:** *"a strip running from **north-east to south-west** direction across **Arunachal
   Pradesh, Upper Assam, Nagaland, Manipur, Mizoram and Tripura upto a height of 1070 metres**"*.
3. ✅ **Belt 3:** *"the **Andaman and Nicobar Islands**"* — **no altitude band printed**; the
   mechanism is **maritime**, not orographic.
4. ⚠️ **Two different ceilings, 1,370 m and 1,070 m**: the North-East lies further **north**, so the
   thermal limit of the tropical class arrives at a **lower** elevation.
5. ⚠️ **The 500 m lower bound**: the orographic maximum is on the **slope**, and the lowland has been
   **cleared** — ⚠️ inference; Khullar prints the bound without a reason.
6. ✅ **Semi-evergreen distribution (longer list):** **Western coast, Assam, lower slopes of the
   Eastern Himalayas, Odisha and Andamans** — it **borders** the wet evergreen areas.
7. ⚠️ **Odisha is in the semi-evergreen list only**, not the wet evergreen list — a close-option
   asymmetry.
8. ✅ **Western Ghats species:** **mesua, white cedar, calophyllum, toon, dhup, palaquium, hopea,
   jamun, canes**.
9. ✅ **Himalayan / North-East species:** **gurjan, chaplasha, jamun, mesua, agar, muli, bamboo**.
10. ⚠️ **Only jamun and mesua are shared**; ✗ **not one equatorial genus** appears; ✅ C2: mahogany is
    *"largely introduced"*.
11. ✅ The timber is *"**fine-grained, hard and durable**"* with *"**high commercial value**"* — the
    problem is not timber quality.
12. ★ ✅ **Not properly exploited due to three reasons:** *"**dense undergrowth, absence of pure
    stands and lack of transport facilities**"*. **"Absence of pure stands" is printed in all three
    books** — Leong p. 120, Majid 5.4, Khullar 169.
13. ✅ **Khullar folio 174, national frame:** about **half** exploitable, about **one fourth**
    potentially exploitable; a large proportion *"**inaccessible** … **non-merchantable**"* in the
    **high Himalayas of Kashmir and Arunachal Pradesh**.
14. ⚠️ **Map-bank rows ABSENT** for **Anaimalai Hills** as an entity, **Agasthyamalai**, **Malabar
    Coast** and **Agumbe**; **PRESENT** for Western Ghats, Nilgiri Hills, Palghat Gap, Silent Valley
    NP, Periyar NP, Eravikulam NP, Namdapha NP, Nameri NP, Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Campbell Bay
    NP, Ten Degree Channel, Great Channel, Indira Point. **Gap declared, not closed** (gate **G25**).

### Concept check

**Question:** State the three belts of India's tropical wet evergreen forest with their printed
altitude limits, and explain why the two montane ceilings differ.

**Model answer:** Khullar gives the **western side of the Western Ghats, 500 to 1,370 metres, south
of Mumbai**; a **north-east to south-west strip across Arunachal Pradesh, Upper Assam, Nagaland,
Manipur, Mizoram and Tripura, up to 1,070 metres**; and the **Andaman and Nicobar Islands**, for
which **no altitude band is printed** because the mechanism there is maritime rather than orographic.
The ceilings differ because the ceiling marks where **temperature falls below the tropical class**:
the North-Eastern belt lies **further north**, so that thermal limit is reached at a **lower**
elevation than in the southern Ghats. Above both lie the montane classes. *(104 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That the forest covers the Ghats as a blanket. It is a **ribbon on the
windward slope** with both a floor and a ceiling; the leeward side is drier and the lowland largely
cleared. *(36 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 15

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** Account for the distribution of India's tropical wet
evergreen forests and explain why, despite valuable timber, they remain little exploited.

**Model:** The distribution is a **mechanism map**. Khullar places the type on the **western side of
the Western Ghats between 500 and 1,370 metres south of Mumbai**, in a **north-east to south-west
strip across Arunachal Pradesh, Upper Assam, Nagaland, Manipur, Mizoram and Tripura up to 1,070
metres**, and in the **Andaman and Nicobar Islands**. The first two mark where orographic
interception of monsoon flow is strongest; the ceilings differ because the more northerly North-East
reaches the thermal limit lower; the islands are wet maritime rather than orographic. Across the
crest lies rain shadow, so the belts are narrow and one-sided. Despite timber that is *"fine-grained,
hard and durable"* with *"high commercial value"*, Khullar records that they are *"not properly
exploited"* owing to **dense undergrowth, absence of pure stands and lack of transport facilities** —
the middle reason being printed also by Leong and Majid for the equatorial selva. *(150 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Three belts with printed altitude bands and the Mumbai cut-off **4** ·
mechanism distinguished for montane and insular belts **2** · ceiling difference explained **1** ·
three non-exploitation reasons with the cross-book convergence **3**

## Lesson 16 — Four numbers that are not the same number: reading a forest statistic

Progress: 16 / 19 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **four-column quantity ledger with definition, denominator and vintage**

### The intuition, before any term

There is no such thing as **"India's forest area"**. There are several different quantities, each
produced by a different method, for a different purpose, at a different date, and they differ from
one another by **hundreds of thousands of square kilometres**.

Ask "how much forest does India have?" and the honest answer begins with a question back: **do you
mean the area the government has legally recorded as forest, or the area a satellite sees tree
canopy on, or the area that a 1968 botanical classification assigns to forest types?** Those are not
three estimates of one number. They are **three different numbers measuring three different things**,
and one of them does not even require trees to be present while another does not require any legal
status at all.

This is the lesson where the edition's statistical discipline is taught explicitly, and it is the
lesson C1 asked for: its lines 197–200 already warn that forest figures require *"the exact FSI
report edition, reference period and mapping definition"* and that *"no national forest-cover figure
is relabelled as evergreen-forest extent"*. **That warning was never turned into teaching. It is
turned into teaching here.**

**Plain words before the terms.** *Forest cover* is what a satellite sees: any patch over one hectare
with tree canopy above a threshold, **whatever it legally is and whoever owns it**. *Recorded forest
area* is what the government's land records say is forest, **whether or not trees are standing on
it**. A *forest type area* is what a botanical classification assigns to a vegetation class. A
*denominator* is what a percentage is divided by, and most forest-statistics errors are denominator
errors.

**Caption.** Four quantities, four definitions, four vintages. Column ④ needs no trees; columns ②
and ③ need no legal status; column ① has its own denominator. Nothing may be subtracted across them.

### ✗ Correction C15 — the denominator trap is built into the printed header

As recorded at **X5**, the printed column header of Khullar **Table 6.1, folio 173** reads
**"Percentage of total area"** — not, as the frozen handoff had it, *"% of total forest area"*. The
audit treated the header as self-labelling. **It is not.** "Total area" invites exactly the reading
that the denominator is India's geographical area, and it is not.

**The arithmetic settles it, and it was re-verified independently at generation:**

| Test | Result |
|---|---|
| Tropical Wet Evergreen **5.120 ÷ 64.000** | **= 8.0 %** — **matches the printed percentage exactly** |
| Tropical Wet Evergreen **5.120 ÷ 328.7** (India, m ha) | **= 1.56 %** — does **not** match |
| All fourteen rows: area ÷ 64, rounded to one decimal | **every row reproduces the printed percentage; no deviations** |
| Column sums | areas **= 64.000 exactly**; percentages **= 100.0 exactly** |

> **The discipline, frozen for this edition.** **Tropical Wet Evergreen is 8.0 % of the 64.000 m ha
> forest-type total, which corresponds to about 1.6 % of India's land area.** The two figures are
> **never** interchanged and **never** quoted without their denominator. ⚠️ And the 64 m ha total is
> itself about **19.5 %** of India's 328.7 m ha — so a percentage from this table is a share of a
> share.

⚠️ **This is the most reusable skill in the lesson and it generalises past this table.** When you meet
any forest percentage, ask **"per cent of what?"** before you ask "is it right?". The printed header
of a textbook table is **not** a reliable statement of its own denominator, and the arithmetic is.

### ✗ Correction C16 — every figure is dated, and the 2003 frame is a historical datum

**✅ Khullar folio 174 (scan PDF 103 left), read directly at generation:**

> "The total geographical area of the country is **32,87,263 sq km** out of which an area of
> **6,77,816 sq km or 20.6 per cent was under forests in 2003 (Table 6.2)**. This is much below the
> average of **30.4 per cent for the world**. … India with about **17.5 per cent of the world's
> population** has nearly **2 per cent of the total forests** … According to the **National Forest
> Policy**, the minimum desired area … for a tropical country like India is about **33 per cent**. As
> per broad policy recommendations, about **60 per cent of the area in the Himalayas and the
> Peninsular hills** and **20 per cent in the Great Plains** should be under forests. Furthermore, the
> **per capita forest area in India is bare 0.06 hectares** against the world average of **1.08
> hectares**."

**✅ And the composition and legal-class frame, same folio:**

| ✅ Printed | Value |
|---|---|
| Legal classes, as shares of total forest area | **Reserved ≈ 54 %**, **Protected ≈ 29 %**, **Unclassed ≈ 17 %** — ✔ sums to 100, verified |
| Private ownership | *"a negligible proportion of **less than 1 per cent**"*; some private forest in **Meghalaya, Odisha, Panjab, Himachal Pradesh** |
| Coniferous forest | **3.5 million hectares**, *"mainly confined to the Himalayan ranges"* — deodar, chir, fir, spruce, pine |
| Broad-leaved forest | *"about **95 per cent** of the total forest area"* |
| Sal and teak | **16.55 %** and **13.82 %** *"of the total area under **broad leaved** forests"* |
| Bamboo | **7.48 %** *"of the total area under **broad leaved** forests"* |
| Other broad-leaved species | **rosewood, Indian laurel, shistram, garyan, benteak** |
| Exploitability | about **half** exploitable; about **one fourth** potentially exploitable |
| Production | **0.5 cubic metre per hectare per year** against a world average of **2.1** |

⚠️ **Note the denominator shift inside the composition rows, because it is a second denominator
trap.** Sal at 16.55 % and teak at 13.82 % are shares of the **broad-leaved** area, **not** of India's
total forest area and certainly not of India. Broad-leaved is itself **about 95 %** of forest area.
**Two nested denominators in one paragraph** — this is why the discipline, not the number, is the
lesson.

**✅ Table 6.2, folio 175 (scan PDF 104), *"India : Forest Cover Estimates (sq km)"*, Source: State
of Forest Report 2005**, read directly at generation:

| ✅ Cycle | ✅ Year | ✅ Satellite and Sensor | ✅ Data Period | ✅ Forest Cover |
|---|---:|---|---|---:|
| First | 1987 | Landsat-Mss | 1981–83 | 640,819 |
| Second | 1989 | Landsat-TM | 1985–87 | 638,804 |
| Third | 1991 | Lansat-TM | 1987–89 | 639,364 |
| Fourth | 1993 | Lansat-TM | 1989–91 | 639,386 |
| Fifth | 1995 | IRS-1B LISS II | 1991–93 | 638,879 |
| Sixth | 1997 | IRS-1B LISS II | 1993–95 | 633,397 |
| Seventh | 1999 | IRS-1C/1D LISS III | 1996–98 | 637,293 |
| Eighth | 2001 | IRS-1C/1D LISS III | 2000 | **675,538** |
| Ninth | 2003 | IRS-1D LISS III | 2002 | **677,816** |

★ **This single table teaches the whole lesson by itself, and it is worth more than any sentence
about methodology.** Three things leap out of it.

1. **The report year is not the data period.** The *"2003"* figure is derived from **2002** data. ✅
   Khullar's own sentence says *"was under forests in 2003"*, and the table's own column says the
   data period is **2002**. **Even inside one table, the date of a figure is ambiguous unless you
   say which date you mean.**
2. **The series is not monotonic and it is not smooth.** Cover **falls** from 640,819 (1987) to
   633,397 (1997), then **rises sharply** to 675,538 (2001) — a jump of about **38,000 km²** in one
   step.
3. **The sensor column explains the jump.** The large step coincides with the move to **IRS-1C/1D
   LISS III** and a changed data period. ⚠️ A change of that size in two years is a **methodological
   artefact far more plausibly than an afforestation event**, and the table gives you the sensor
   column precisely so you can see it. **This is the empirical reason why cross-vintage arithmetic is
   forbidden: the instrument changed.**

**Arithmetic verified independently at generation:** 99,779 + 306,890 + 308,674 = **715,343** ✔ ;
715,343 + 112,014 = **827,357** ✔ ; 715,343 ÷ 3,287,263 = **21.76 %** ✔ ; 827,357 ÷ 3,287,263 =
**25.17 %** ✔ ; 112,014 ÷ 3,287,263 = **3.41 %** ✔.

⚠️ **The density-class split is the single most answer-useful fact in the block.** Very Dense Forest
is **99,779 km²** out of **715,343 km²** — about **one-seventh** of forest cover (an internal ratio
within one vintage, which is permitted). **Open Forest alone exceeds Very Dense Forest threefold.**
"India has 21.76 per cent forest cover" and "India has 21.76 per cent dense forest" are **completely
different claims**, and only the first is true.

> ⛔ **REFUSAL, STATED EXPLICITLY.** **No arithmetic is performed across the four quantities anywhere
> in this edition.** The 1994 type-area, the 2003 cover, the 2023 cover and the recorded forest area
> stand side by side and **no difference, ratio, trend, gap or rate of change is computed between
> them**. Sentences of the form *"forest cover has risen by X since Khullar"* or *"X km² of recorded
> forest has no tree cover"* are **not written here** (**refusal class 6**, gate **G12**). Internal
> ratios **within one vintage** — the density classes within ISFR 2023, the rows within Table 6.1 —
> are permitted and are used above.

### 📰 The methodological debate, reported

📰 **Reported, 2026.** The ISFR methodology is under sustained public criticism on grounds that bear
directly on everything this edition teaches about **belukar** and about forest **type**. Reported
criticisms include: that the operative criterion — **any patch over one hectare with canopy density
above 10 per cent, irrespective of ownership, legal status, or whether the trees are native or
planted** — causes **plantations, orchards, coconut and bamboo groves, rubber, and shade trees in
tea and coffee estates** to be counted as "forest" alongside natural forest; that reported gains lie
substantially **outside recorded forest areas** and are attributed largely to plantations; and that
**degradation of dense and moderately dense forest into open forest** is masked by plantation gains
elsewhere (reported by The Hindu, Frontline, NE Now and others, 2026, including a reported open
letter from former civil servants).

⚠️ **How this edition uses that reporting, and how far.** It is carried as **📰 reported debate with
named outlets and a date**, not as a finding, and **no revised figure is substituted for an official
one**. Its relevance to Topic 15 is **conceptual and precise**: a canopy-density threshold
**cannot distinguish** a primary wet evergreen stand from **belukar-equivalent secondary regrowth**,
or from a **rubber plantation** — and Lessons 9 and 11 established that both of those are
structurally forest-like and ecologically very different. **So "forest cover" and "evergreen forest
extent" are not merely different numbers; they are different kinds of thing.** That is exactly what
C1 lines 197–200 warned against, and it is why the warning was worth promoting into a lesson. ↪
CAMPA, the Forest (Conservation) Act and compensatory afforestation belong to `Env-Ecology/basic/12`,
which owns **2019 Prelims Q68**.

### Causal chain, assumptions and limits

```text
  A QUESTION: "how much forest does India have?"
        │
        ├──► ask: BY WHAT DEFINITION?
        │         ├─ a botanical CLASSIFICATION of vegetation types  ──► ①
        │         ├─ a REMOTE-SENSED canopy-density threshold        ──► ②③
        │         └─ a LEGAL land-record category                    ──► ④
        │
        ├──► ask: AT WHAT DATE?
        │         ├─ ⏱ 1994 (Sagreiya, via Khullar)                  ──► ①
        │         ├─ ⏱ 2003 report / 2002 DATA PERIOD                ──► ②
        │         └─ ⏱ ISFR 2023, released Dec 2024                  ──► ③④
        │
        ├──► ask: BY WHAT INSTRUMENT?
        │         └─ ✅ Table 6.2's SENSOR column: Landsat-MSS → TM →
        │            IRS-1B LISS II → IRS-1C/1D LISS III
        │                 │
        │                 ▼
        │            ⚠️ the ~38,000 km² step between 1999 and 2001
        │               coincides with a SENSOR AND DATA-PERIOD CHANGE
        │                 │
        │                 ▼
        │            ⛔ THEREFORE NO CROSS-VINTAGE ARITHMETIC
        │
        └──► ask: PER CENT OF WHAT?
                  ├─ ✅ Table 6.1 % → of its own 64.000 m ha column total
                  ├─ ✅ sal/teak/bamboo % → of the BROAD-LEAVED area
                  └─ 📰 ISFR % → of India's 3,287,263 km²
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ⚠️ AND THE CATEGORY POINT THAT MATTERS MOST FOR THIS TOPIC:
     a canopy-density threshold CANNOT distinguish
         primary wet evergreen │ secondary regrowth │ rubber plantation
     ──► ❗ A NATIONAL FOREST-COVER FIGURE IS NEVER EVERGREEN-FOREST EXTENT
         (C1 lines 197–200, promoted here into taught discipline)
```

**Assumptions.** That each source reports what it says it reports; that the search-mediated figures
faithfully reproduce the primary document — which **could not be verified against the primary PDF**
and is disclosed as such.

**What this chain does NOT explain.** It does not explain **FSI's mapping methodology in detail**,
which was not retrievable. It does not supply a **current type-wise area** — ⚠️ **U4**, declared and
not filled. It does not adjudicate the **reported criticism**, which is carried as reported. And it
does not teach **state-wise rankings**, **mangrove cover**, **CAMPA** or **carbon accounting**, all
of which are routed.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

**Comparison — the same country, four answers.**

| Question | Answer | Quantity |
|---|---|---|
| How much land is **classified into forest types**? | **64.000 m ha = 640,000 km²** ⏱ 1994 | ① |
| How much land did a satellite see **canopy** on? | **677,816 km²** ⏱ 2003 (data 2002) | ② |
| How much land does a satellite see **canopy** on now? | **715,343 km²** ⏱ ISFR 2023 📰 | ③ |
| How much land is **legally recorded** as forest? | **775,288 km²** ⏱ ISFR 2023 📰 | ④ |

**Objection.** "These are obviously estimates of the same thing at different times. Refusing to
compare them is excessive caution that loses information."

**Reply.** They are **not** estimates of the same thing, and three independent facts establish it.
**First, by definition**: ④ is a **legal** category that requires **no trees**, while ② and ③ are
**physical** categories that require **no legal status** — an orchard counts for ③ and not for ④, and
a denuded reserved forest counts for ④ and not for ③. They can diverge without either being wrong.
**Second, by instrument**: Table 6.2's own sensor column shows cover jumping about **38,000 km²**
between 1999 and 2001 across a sensor and data-period change — **a measured difference that is not a
physical change**. If a two-year step inside one consistent series is an artefact, a twenty-year step
across two different publications is not interpretable at all. **Third, by category**: a
canopy-density threshold cannot separate primary evergreen forest from secondary regrowth or from a
rubber plantation, which is the substance of the 2026 reported criticism. Comparing ① with ③ would be
comparing a **botanical classification** with a **reflectance threshold**. **The refusal preserves
information; it is the subtraction that would destroy it.**

**Unresolved residual.** ⚠️ Three gaps stand open and are not filled. **U3**: the primary FSI and PIB
sources could not be reached at audit or at generation, so every ISFR figure here is **📰
search-mediated** and must be re-verified against the FSI primary document when access is restored.
**U4**: no **current official type-wise area table** exists in hand, so the only type-wise breakdown
available remains **Sagreiya 1994** via Khullar. **U6**: reported regional declines — in the Western
Ghats, the Himalayan States and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands — come from **secondary press
summaries**, are carried as 📰 reported only, and are **never stated as findings**.

### India hook — Andaman and Nicobar at over 80 per cent, twice

🇮🇳 **The hook.** ✅ **Khullar folio 175** records that *"**Andaman Nicobar Islands, Arunachal
Pradesh, Mizoram and Nagaland** are very rich areas. These regions have **over 80 per cent** of their
geographical area under forests."* And 📰 **ISFR 2023** reports **Andaman and Nicobar at 81.62 %**.
Two assessments, roughly two decades apart, both placing the islands above eighty per cent.

**The stated limitation — and this is the hook's whole purpose.** **The agreement is coincidental,
not continuity.** These are **two different assessments** with different sensors, different
definitions and different dates, and ⛔ **no trend, difference or rate of change may be read from
their proximity** (**refusal class 6**). The correct use is **corroborative**, not arithmetical: it
is reasonable to say that *"both Khullar's book-era figure and ISFR 2023 place the Andaman and
Nicobar Islands above 80 per cent forest cover"*; it is **not** permissible to say that cover *"has
remained stable"*, *"has increased by 1.62 points"* or *"has barely changed"*. ⚠️ And note that
Khullar's over-80 list has **four** members while the ISFR reported ranking puts **Lakshadweep
(91.33 %)** and **Mizoram (85.34 %)** above the islands — ↪ **state-wise rankings are routed to
`Env-Ecology/basic/11`, which owns 2019 Prelims Q33**, and are **named here only as corroboration of
Khullar's list**, never taught as a ranking.

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Treating "forest cover" and "recorded forest area" as the same.** ④ **requires no trees**; ②
  and ③ **require no legal status**.
- ❌ **Reading Table 6.1's percentages as shares of India.** The denominator is its own **64.000
  m ha** total — proved by arithmetic (**X5**).
- ❌ **Reading sal 16.55 % and teak 13.82 % as shares of forest area.** They are shares of the
  **broad-leaved** area, which is itself about **95 %** of forest area.
- ❌ **Computing anything across the four quantities.** ⛔ **Refusal class 6**, gate **G12**.
- ❌ **Confusing report year with data period.** Khullar's *"2003"* figure has a data period of
  **2002**, printed in his own table.
- ❌ **Reading the 1999→2001 jump as afforestation.** It coincides with a **sensor and data-period
  change**.
- ❌ **Quoting any ISFR figure bare.** Every one carries **edition, reference period, retrieval date
  and class marker**, and the **primary retrieval failed** (**U3**).
- ❌ **Saying "India has 21.76 per cent dense forest".** VDF is **99,779 km²** of **715,343 km²**;
  Open Forest alone is over three times VDF.
- ❌ **Relabelling national forest cover as evergreen-forest extent.** ❗ **C1 lines 197–200**, and
  the reason is categorial, not arithmetical.
- ❌ **Teaching state rankings or mangrove cover.** ↪ `Env-Ecology/basic/11` (**2019 Prelims Q33**)
  and `Env-Ecology/basic/24`.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** GS-I, *"distribution of key natural resources across … the Indian subcontinent"* —
the evidentiary discipline required to state the extent of India's forest resource.

**PYQ position.** **Topic 15 owns no routed PYQ for this content; this lesson supports
`Env-Ecology/basic/11`** (state-wise forest-cover rankings, **2019 Prelims Q33**),
**`Env-Ecology/basic/24`** (mangrove cover, **2019 Mains GS-I Q5** and **2026 Prelims Q23**) and
**`Env-Ecology/basic/12`** (CAMPA, **2019 Prelims Q68**) — **none of which it answers**. Topic 15's
own demands remain at `_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` lines **453** and **666**, both `Routed;
key unavailable locally`. **No key is inferred anywhere, and 2026 provisional keys are never cited.**

**Answer use.** Open any forest-statistics answer by **naming the quantity**, then give the figure
with its vintage. *"India's forest statistics are four different quantities. Forest cover — what a
satellite sees above a 10 per cent canopy threshold, irrespective of ownership or legal status — is
715,343 km² or 21.76 per cent in ISFR 2023, released December 2024, of which Very Dense Forest is
99,779 km², Moderately Dense 306,890 km² and Open 308,674 km². Recorded forest area, a legal category
requiring no trees, is 775,288 km². Khullar's book-era figure of 677,816 km² or 20.6 per cent belongs
to 2003 with a 2002 data period, and his Table 6.1's 64 million hectares is a 1994 forest-type area
whose percentages are shares of that total, not of India."*

### Revision points

1. **Four quantities, four definitions, four vintages:** ① **64.000 m ha** forest-type area ⏱1994 ·
   ② **677,816 km² = 20.6 %** forest cover ⏱2003 (data 2002) · ③ **715,343 km² = 21.76 %** forest
   cover ⏱ISFR 2023 · ④ **775,288 km²** recorded forest area ⏱ISFR 2023.
2. ❗ ④ **requires no trees**; ② and ③ **require no legal status**. They are different **kinds** of
   quantity.
3. ⛔ **No arithmetic across the four.** No difference, ratio, trend or gap (**refusal class 6**).
   Internal ratios **within one vintage** are permitted.
4. ✗ **X5 / C15:** Table 6.1's printed header is *"**Percentage of total area**"*; the arithmetic
   proves the denominator is its **own 64.000 m ha** total. **5.120 ÷ 64 = 8.0**; **5.120 ÷ 328.7 =
   1.56**.
5. **Tropical Wet Evergreen = 8.0 % of forest-type area ≈ 1.6 % of India's land area.** Never
   interchange. 64 m ha is itself about **19.5 %** of India.
6. ⚠️ **Second denominator trap:** sal **16.55 %**, teak **13.82 %**, bamboo **7.48 %** are shares of
   the **broad-leaved** area, which is itself **about 95 %** of forest area.
7. ✅ **Khullar's 2003 frame:** geographical area **3,287,263 km²**; **677,816 km² = 20.6 %**; world
   average **30.4 %**; policy target **33 %**, with **60 %** in the Himalayas and Peninsular hills
   and **20 %** in the Great Plains; per-capita **0.06 ha** against world **1.08 ha**; production
   **0.5 m³/ha/yr** against world **2.1**.
8. ✅ **Legal classes:** Reserved **≈54 %**, Protected **≈29 %**, Unclassed **≈17 %** — sums to 100,
   verified. Private **under 1 %**.
9. ★ ✅ **Table 6.2** gives **nine cycles, 1987–2003**, with **sensor** and **data-period** columns:
   **640,819 → 677,816 km²**, **non-monotonic**, with a **~38,000 km² jump** between 1999 and 2001
   across a **sensor and data-period change**. ⚠️ **The instrument changed; that is why cross-vintage
   arithmetic is forbidden.**
10. ⚠️ **Report year ≠ data period**, even inside one table: *"2003"* rests on **2002** data.
11. 📰 **ISFR 2023 density classes:** VDF (≥70 %) **99,779** · MDF (≥40 %, <70 %) **306,890** · OF
    (≥10 %, <40 %) **308,674** — sum **715,343** ✔. Tree cover **112,014 (3.41 %)**; forest + tree
    **827,357 (25.17 %)** ✔.
12. ⚠️ **VDF is about one-seventh of forest cover; Open Forest alone exceeds VDF threefold.**
    "21.76 per cent forest cover" is **not** "21.76 per cent dense forest".
13. 📰 **U5 CLOSED at generation:** ISFR 2023, 18th edition, released December 2024, remains the
    latest as at 2026-10-04. ⚠️ **U3 PERSISTS:** fsi.nic.in failed at TLS and PIB returned 403/401;
    all ISFR figures are **search-mediated** and must be re-verified against the primary PDF.
14. 📰 **Reported 2026 critique:** the ≥10 % canopy criterion counts **plantations, orchards, bamboo,
    rubber and tea/coffee shade trees** as forest; gains reported largely **outside recorded forest
    areas**; dense-to-open degradation reported as masked. Carried as **reported**, with outlets and
    date.
15. ❗ **A canopy-density threshold cannot distinguish primary evergreen forest from secondary
    regrowth or from a plantation.** Therefore **no national forest-cover figure is ever relabelled
    as evergreen-forest extent** (C1 lines 197–200, promoted into discipline).

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "India's recorded forest area is 775,288 km² while its forest cover
is 715,343 km², so about 60,000 km² of recorded forest has lost its trees." Identify every error.

**Model answer:** The subtraction is **impermissible** and its interpretation is **unsupported**.
The two are **different kinds of quantity**: recorded forest area is a **legal** category requiring
**no trees**, while forest cover is a **physical** canopy measure requiring **no legal status** — so
an orchard enters one and not the other, and the two sets only partly overlap. A difference between
them is therefore **not a measure of tree loss**. Khullar's own Table 6.2 shows cover jumping about
38,000 km² across a **sensor change**, proving that differences can be artefacts. Each figure must be
stated with its **definition, denominator and vintage**, and **no arithmetic performed across them**.
*(104 words)*

**Misconception to avoid:** That two numbers in the same units measure the same thing. Units are not
definitions; the question to ask of any forest figure is what it counts, by what rule, on what date.
*(37 words)*

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 16

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** "A forest statistic is meaningless without its
definition, denominator and date." Examine with reference to India's forest data.

**Model:** India yields **four** non-interchangeable quantities. Khullar's **Table 6.1**, after
**Sagreiya (1994)**, assigns **64.000 million hectares** to Champion–Seth forest **types**; its
percentages are shares of **that** total, so Tropical Wet Evergreen's **8.0 %** corresponds to about
**1.6 %** of India's land area. Khullar's folio 174 gives **677,816 km² or 20.6 %** under forests
**in 2003** — from a **2002** data period, printed in his own Table 6.2. ISFR 2023, released
**December 2024**, reports **forest cover 715,343 km² (21.76 %)**, comprising **Very Dense 99,779**,
**Moderately Dense 306,890** and **Open 308,674 km²**, and separately a **recorded forest area of
775,288 km²** — a legal category requiring no trees. Table 6.2's own sensor column shows cover
jumping about **38,000 km²** across an instrument change, so differences across vintages are
artefacts as often as events. Hence no national cover figure is evergreen-forest extent.
*(150 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Four quantities distinguished by definition **4** · denominator discipline on
Table 6.1 **2** · vintage and data-period discipline **2** · sensor-change evidence used to justify
refusing cross-vintage arithmetic **2**

Progress: 17 / 19 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **ownership boundary fence diagram, with each routed item placed on its side**

### The intuition, before any term

This lesson is unlike the others. It is not mainly about forests; it is about **where this topic
stops**.

Biodiversity is the most seductive drift in the whole Geography syllabus. You are teaching evergreen
forest; evergreen forest is species-rich; species-richness invites hotspots; hotspots invite
conservation status; conservation status invites protected areas, CAMPA and REDD+. Four steps and
you are writing an Environment answer on a Geography paper — and, worse, **answering questions that
the ledgers assign to other owners**.

So the discipline is simple to state and hard to keep. **Geography owns richness as a consequence of
the physical environment. Ecology owns richness as a conservation object.** The same forest, the
same species, two different questions. This lesson draws that line, puts each item on its side of
it, and gives you the one thing Geography is entitled to say.

**Plain words before the terms.** *Endemic* means **found nowhere else** — an endemic species of the
Western Ghats occurs in the Western Ghats and in no other region on Earth. *Micro-endemic* means
restricted to a very small area indeed. A *cryptic species* is one that looks almost identical to
another but is genetically or behaviourally distinct.

### The primary visual — the boundary fence
