# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Seven original questions with complete models and **seven pairwise-distinct paper-level rubrics**,
scoring at the paper level rather than at the lesson level: **structure**, **causal completeness**,
**map use**, **data discipline**, **source attribution**, **counter-argument handling** and
**conclusion quality**. Together with the nineteen lesson-local rubrics these make **twenty-six
pairwise-distinct dimension-sets**. Word caps are hard: **10-mark ≤ 150 words; 15- and 20-mark ≤ 250
words.**

## Q1 — 10 marks, 150 words — rubric dimension: STRUCTURE

**Question.** Examine why the hot, wet equatorial climate supports the world's most luxuriant
vegetation yet has historically resisted dense settlement and large-scale commercial forestry.

**Model.** The same properties that maximise plant growth obstruct human use. Continuous warmth and
moisture remove every limit on growth, giving a three-level canopy with trees *"over 150 feet"* and,
in Leong's Malaysian estimate, *"200 species of trees … in an acre"*. **That richness is the
obstacle.** Trees *"do not occur in homogenous stands"*, *"there are no frozen surfaces to facilitate
logging"*, and hardwoods are *"too heavy to float in the rivers"* — so extraction is *"a most
difficult task"* and *"many tropical countries are net timber importers"*. Settlement faces a second
set: the nutrient capital sits in the biomass, so *"the torrential downpours soon wash out most of the
soil nutrients"* after clearance; livestock is *"greatly handicapped by an absence of meadow grass"*;
and relative humidity *"over 80 per cent"* imposes real physiological cost. **Java's volcanic ashes
show the constraint is a soil regime, not a latitude.** *(148 words)*

**Rubric — STRUCTURE (10):** Thesis that richness and obstruction share a cause, stated at the top
**3** · two constraint sets separated and not merged **3** · each claim carrying its printed quotation
**2** · counter-case placed last as a limit rather than an afterthought **2**

## Q2 — 10 marks, 150 words — rubric dimension: DATA DISCIPLINE

**Question.** "India's forest statistics cannot be compared across sources." Examine.

**Model.** They cannot, because they are **four different quantities**. Khullar's **Table 6.1**,
after **Sagreiya (1994)**, assigns **64.000 million hectares** to Champion–Seth forest **types**,
with percentages computed on **that** total — Tropical Wet Evergreen's **8.0 %** is about **1.6 %**
of India's land area. Khullar's folio 174 reports **677,816 km² or 20.6 %** under forests **in
2003**, from a **2002** data period printed in his own Table 6.2. **ISFR 2023**, released **December
2024**, reports **forest cover 715,343 km² (21.76 %)** — **Very Dense 99,779**, **Moderately Dense
306,890**, **Open 308,674 km²** — and separately **recorded forest area 775,288 km²**, a legal
category requiring no trees. Table 6.2's own sensor column shows cover jumping about **38,000 km²**
across an instrument change, so inter-vintage differences are artefacts as readily as events.
**Each figure is usable; no difference between them is.** *(149 words)*

**Rubric — DATA DISCIPLINE (10):** All four quantities named with their definitions **3** ·
denominator correctly identified for Table 6.1 **2** · vintage and data-period distinction **2** ·
sensor-change evidence deployed **2** · explicit refusal of cross-vintage arithmetic **1**

## Q3 — 15 marks, 250 words — rubric dimension: CAUSAL COMPLETENESS

**Question.** Account for the rainfall regime of the hot, wet equatorial climate and explain why its
vegetation is evergreen while comparably wet Indian regions carry deciduous forest.

**Model.** Equatorial rainfall is produced by **four** mechanisms operating at different scales.
**Locally**, bright sunny mornings drive heavy evaporation and convectional currents, giving
afternoon downpours from *"towering cumulo-nimbus clouds"* so intense that one afternoon's fall *"may
be as much as the deserts receive for the entire year"*. **Synoptically**, Leong records
*"intermittent showers from cyclonic atmospheric disturbances caused by the convergence of air
currents in the Doldrums"* — organised systems independent of local heating and of the hour.
**Orographically**, *"mountainous regions also experience much orographic or relief rain"*.
**Seasonally**, the sun's twice-yearly overhead passage modulates the first two, producing lagged
maxima *"in April and October"* and least rain *"at the June and December solstices"*. Their
**redundancy** is decisive: no single mechanism runs continuously, so only their overlap yields
*"no month without rain"*.

Evergreenness follows from **distribution, not total**. Khullar defines evergreen trees as those that
*"do not shed their leaves annually, at least not together"* — de-synchronisation, not permanence —
and that requires a dry season *"distinctly short"*. **Kuala Lumpur receives 95 inches**; many Indian
monsoon stations receive as much or more, yet **Mawsynram** and **Cherrapunji** concentrate enormous
totals into one season, forcing synchronised leaf fall and producing deciduous forest. Khullar's own
Indian ladder is a threshold ladder for that reason: **above 200 cm evergreen, 100–200 cm monsoon
deciduous**. Leong's chapter sets the comparison himself, contrasting **Alor Star** with **Bombay**.
*(250 words)*

**Rubric — CAUSAL COMPLETENESS (15):** All four mechanisms with their scales **4** · redundancy
argued as the reason for a no-dry-month regime **3** · de-synchronisation definition of evergreenness
**3** · total-versus-distribution demonstrated with a numerical counter-case **3** · the book's own
comparison deployed **2**

## Q4 — 15 marks, 250 words — rubric dimension: SOURCE ATTRIBUTION

**Question.** Discuss the structure and floristics of tropical rain forest, distinguishing carefully
between what the standard texts state and what is standard ecological inference.

**Model.** **What the texts state.** Leong's **Fig. 123** is captioned *"Sketch to show the **three
distinct layers** of an equatorial forest"*, with an upper tier *"over 150 feet"* of *"slender trunks
[that] pierce skywards with wide-spread branches at the top"*, an intermediate tier at **60 feet**,
and a ground of *"ferns and herbaceous plants which can tolerate shade"*. Majid independently records
that *"the rain forest canopy form **three levels**"*, the upper *"not continuous"*, the middle *"the
most continuous"*, the lower of *"seedlings, ferns, bamboo"*, with trunks *"buttressed by large
wall-like flanks"* and *"no branches for at least the lower two-thirds"*. Leong's hardwoods are
**mahogany, ebony, greenheart, cabinet woods and dyewoods**; Majid's are **mahogany, ebony and
rosewood**, with **balsa** as the light exception. Climbers are *"lianas or rattan … hundreds of feet
long"* with cords *"up to 20 cm in diameter"*; epiphytes — **orchids, bromeliads, ferns** — are
*"supported physically but not nutritionally"*. Leong adds that *"because the trees cut out most of
the sunlight **the undergrowth is not dense**"*.

**What is inference.** That light-sorting produces the tiering; that tiering multiplies niches; and
the modern **five-stratum** scheme, which neither book uses. **The count itself is a convention**:
foliage is distributed continuously, as spaceborne LiDAR profiles now show. Attributing five strata,
or **rosewood**, to Leong is a misattribution; rosewood is Majid's. *(250 words)*

**Rubric — SOURCE ATTRIBUTION (15):** Three-level scheme quoted from **both** books **4** · hardwood
lists correctly assigned to their respective books **3** · inference layer explicitly separated **3**
· the open-floor statement retained **2** · convention-versus-measurement point on the stratum count
**3**

## Q5 — 15 marks, 250 words — rubric dimension: COUNTER-ARGUMENT HANDLING

**Question.** "Shifting cultivation is a destructive practice that should be eliminated." Critically
examine.

**Model.** The proposition misidentifies both the mechanism and the fault. **The mechanism.** In the
humid tropics fertility is held in **biomass, not soil**: Leong records that *"it is a misconception
that tropical soils are rich"*, that heavy leaf-fall and bacterial decomposition give a humus mantle
making the soil *"fairly fertile"*, and that *"once the humus is used and the natural vegetative cover
is removed, the torrential downpours soon wash out most of the soil nutrients"*. Burning a **ladang**
transfers the standing capital into ash a crop root can reach, giving the *"heavy croppings"* he
observes; two or three crops exhaust it, and *"when the fertility is exhausted, the clearing is
abandoned"*. The **belukar** regrowth is not waste — it is the operation that **rebuilds the
capital**. Shifting cultivation is therefore a **correct design** for a system that cannot bank
nutrients in soil.

**The counter-argument has force, but it locates the fault wrongly.** Degradation is real: shortened
fallows leave the capital unrebuilt, yields fall, and *"lalang (tall grass) … may choke crops and
overwhelm estates"*. But the failing variable is **fallow length**, not the technique, and fallow
length is driven by land scarcity and tenure. **Elimination would remove a system without removing
its cause.** Reported policy accordingly aims at **transforming rather than eliminating** jhum.

**Conclusion.** Judge the fallow, not the fire. *(248 words)*

**Rubric — COUNTER-ARGUMENT HANDLING (15):** Mechanism established before evaluation **4** ·
counter-argument stated at its strongest **3** · fault relocated to the variable with evidence **4** ·
policy implication derived from the diagnosis **2** · the correct target named in the conclusion
**2**

## Q6 — 20 marks, 250 words — rubric dimension: MAP USE

**Question.** Describe the distribution of India's tropical wet evergreen and semi-evergreen forests
and account for it.

**Model.** **Wet evergreen** occupies three belts. First, the **western side of the Western Ghats,
between 500 and 1,370 metres, south of Mumbai** — windward to the south-west monsoon, with the
leeward Deccan side rain-shadowed. Second, a **north-east to south-west strip across Arunachal
Pradesh, Upper Assam, Nagaland, Manipur, Mizoram and Tripura, up to 1,070 metres** — intercepting the
Bay of Bengal branch funnelled up the Brahmaputra lowland. Third, the **Andaman and Nicobar
Islands**, for which no altitude band is printed, because the control there is **maritime** rather
than orographic. **Semi-evergreen** borders these in drier settings — **the Western coast, Assam,
the lower slopes of the Eastern Himalayas, Odisha and the Andamans**; note that **Odisha appears for
semi-evergreen only**.

**Accounting.** The belts are a **mechanism map**: they mark where orographic interception is
strongest. The **500 m floor** reflects both lowland clearance and the fact that enhancement occurs
on the slope; the **ceilings differ** — 1,370 m against 1,070 m — because the more northerly
North-Eastern belt reaches the thermal limit of the tropical class at a lower elevation. Thresholds
follow: wet evergreen above **250 cm** with humidity above **77 %**; semi-evergreen **200–250 cm**
with about **75 %**. Despite timber *"fine-grained, hard and durable"*, these forests are *"not
properly exploited"* owing to **dense undergrowth, absence of pure stands and lack of transport
facilities**. *(249 words)*

**Rubric — MAP USE (20):** Three belts located with printed altitude bands and the Mumbai cut-off
**6** · windward–leeward asymmetry stated **3** · semi-evergreen distribution given separately with
the Odisha asymmetry **4** · mechanism distinguished for montane and insular belts **4** · ceiling
difference explained **2** · non-exploitation reasons appended **1**

## Q7 — 20 marks, 250 words — rubric dimension: CONCLUSION QUALITY

**Question.** "India's equatorial-type evergreen forests are best understood as an analogue, not an
instance." Critically evaluate, and assess what follows for the way their extent should be reported.

**Model.** **The analogue holds on five dimensions.** Architecture: Leong's *"three distinct
layers"* and trees *"over 150 feet"* — **45.7 m** — against Khullar's *"lofty, very dense
multilayered"* stands that *"often reach 45 metres"*. Life-forms: hardwoods, canes, bamboos, ferns,
climbers and epiphytic orchids on both sides. Humidity: *"over 80 per cent"* against *"above 77 per
cent"*. Nutrients: a leaching regime with capital in biomass. Economics: **"absence of pure stands"
is printed in all three books**.

**It fails on climate.** India has no *"month without rain"*; its annual temperature range far
exceeds the **0.4–1.8 °C** of Leong's seven stations; and of his four rainfall mechanisms only
**relief rain** operates, seasonally. **And on flora: not one genus is shared.**

**What follows for reporting extent is strict.** Because the Indian forest is defined by a **1968
classification** with contested thresholds — Khullar sets evergreen at **200 cm** on folio 168 and
above **250 cm** on folio 169, unreconciled — its extent is a **class area**, not a physical
measurement. The only type-wise figure available is **Sagreiya's 1994** table, where wet evergreen is
**5.120 m ha, 8.0 % of a 64 m ha total** and about **1.6 %** of India. **National forest cover is a
different quantity entirely** and can never be relabelled as evergreen extent, since a canopy
threshold cannot separate primary forest from regrowth or plantation.

**Conclusion.** The resemblance is structural and real; the identification is false; and the extent
is reportable only with its class, its denominator and its date. *(250 words)*

**Rubric — CONCLUSION QUALITY (20):** Analogue established on named dimensions with both sides cited
**6** · climatic failure specified with printed values **4** · flora disanalogy supplied as the
critical move **3** · consequence for reporting derived rather than appended **4** · conclusion
grading resemblance, identification and reportability separately **3**

# REMEDIATION

Twenty-one errors, each matched to the correction that governs it, the diagnosis of **why** it
happens, and a drill that fixes it. The first eleven are **owner-inherited** — they are in the
canonical notes and will be reproduced by anyone who trusts the tags. The remaining ten are
**generic failure modes** of this topic.

## Part A — Owner-inherited errors

| # | Error as it appears | Correction | Diagnosis — why it happens | Drill |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | *"The diurnal range exceeds the annual range"* under a book mark, four times | **C1** | A true inference was repeated until repetition felt like provenance | Write Leong's sentence from memory: *"The diurnal range of temperature is small, **and so is** the annual range."* Then add the ordering **as ⚠️** in a separate line |
| R2 | *"about 5°–10° N/S"* quoted as the definition | **C2** | The owner quoted **one** of three places the book speaks | Say aloud: *"p. 116 says 5°–10°, p. 114 says 0°–10°, Fig. 121 shades a continuous belt."* Three locations, one breath |
| R3 | *"150–250 cm"* under a book mark | **C3** | A rounded conversion acquired the status of a reading | Always write **"60–100 inches (152–254 cm)"**. Never the rounded band alone |
| R4 | *"26–28 °C"* as Leong's figure | **C4** | Converted band replacing a single printed Fahrenheit value | Write **"always around 80 °F"**; add Majid's **"around 25 °C"** beside it; average neither |
| R5 | *"annual range <3 °C"* used as though tight | **C5** | A safe ceiling mistaken for the evidence | Recite the ladder: **Quito 0.7 · Jaluit 0.8 · Djakarta 1.8 · KL ≤2 · Bogota 2 · Singapore 2.3 · Colombo 3.2** °F |
| R6 | *"slight double maxima near the equinoxes"* | **C6** | A hedge added and the months dropped | Write **April and October**, *"shortly **after** the equinoxes"*, and **June and December** minima |
| R7 | *"mahogany, ebony and rosewood"* in a Leong-framed section | **C7** | Two books' lists merged under one attribution | Two columns: Leong = **mahogany, ebony, greenheart, cabinet woods, dyewoods**; Majid = **mahogany, ebony, rosewood** |
| R8 | A six-row stratification table under a book mark | **C8** | A modern scheme felt more complete than the printed one | Recite the **Fig. 123 caption** and Majid's **"form three levels"**; then label any five-stratum scheme **⚠️** |
| R9 | *"the biggest drawback is inaccessibility"* | **C9** | A plausible obstacle displaced three printed ones; the word was imported from Khullar's **Indian** passage | Apply the survival test: build a perfect road — do the obstacles vanish? **No homogenous stands**, **no frozen surfaces**, **will not float** all survive |
| R10 | *"Large-scale livestock farming does not occur"* | **C10** | A trap line over-rendered *"handicapped"* into absence, contradicting three other lines of the same file | Memorise the **frozen formulation**: traditional pastoralism **severely handicapped**; **post-clearance ranching occurs** and drives deforestation |
| R11 | *"wet evergreen … exceeds about 200–250 cm"* | **C11** | Two adjacent classes merged, destroying the discriminator | Two rows, three criteria: **Wet >250 cm / 25–27 °C / >77 %**; **Semi 200–250 cm / 24–27 °C / ~75 %** |

## Part B — Generic failure modes of this topic

| # | Error | Correction | Diagnosis | Drill |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| R12 | Silently reconciling Khullar's 200 cm and 250 cm | **C12** | Tidiness mistaken for accuracy | Say both folios and the word **unresolved**. Offer the reconciliation **as ⚠️ reader's inference** only |
| R13 | Reading Table 6.1's percentages as shares of India | **C15**, **X5** | The printed header *"Percentage of total area"* is genuinely ambiguous | Compute: **5.120 ÷ 64 = 8.0**; **5.120 ÷ 328.7 = 1.56**. The arithmetic names the denominator |
| R14 | Quoting an ISFR figure bare | **C16** | Current figures feel self-dating | Attach four things every time: **edition · reference period · retrieval date · class marker** |
| R15 | Computing a difference across the four forest quantities | **H12**, refusal 6 | They share units, so they look comparable | Recite what each one **counts**: a class, a canopy, a canopy, a **land record**. Then recall the **38,000 km² sensor jump** |
| R16 | Repeating *"Ghana and Nigeria"* or *"Malaysia and Indonesia each over a third"* as current | **C17**, **C18** | Book sentences carry no visible expiry | Stamp **⏱1970s-vintage**, state that the ranking has changed, name the owner, and stop |
| R17 | Quoting C2's jhum fallow figures as book-grounded | **C19**, **U2** | "Earlier verified conversion" reads like a citation | Ask: **which document, which page?** If the answer names a **process**, the tag is ⚠️ |
| R18 | Importing the laterite profile into the nutrient lesson | **C20** | Leaching and laterite feel like one subject | Teach the **process**; route the **soil type**. Leong never writes *laterite* |
| R19 | Teaching equatorial rainfall as convection alone | **C21**, **H14** | The owner taught one mechanism and it was sufficient-sounding | Count to four: **convection · doldrum convergence · relief · equinox lag**, all on printed p. 117 |
| R20 | Supplying an ESA percentage | **U8**, refusal 3 | A contrast invites two numbers | Say the structural reason: **different methods, different landscape definitions, not interchangeable** |
| R21 | Drifting into monsoon, conservation or commodity rankings | §10 route-outs | The adjacent material is genuinely interesting | Before each paragraph ask: **is this a physical cause, or a policy/conservation/market claim?** |

## The six discriminations this topic is actually tested on

> 1. **Total against distribution.** Mawsynram beats Kuala Lumpur on total and loses on the
>    criterion.
> 2. **Diurnal against annual.** Both small; the ordering is ⚠️ inference, not a book sentence.
> 3. **Wet evergreen against semi-evergreen.** **>250 cm / >77 %** against **200–250 cm / ~75 %**.
> 4. **Forest cover against recorded forest area.** One needs trees; the other needs a land record.
> 5. **Mature forest against secondary forest.** Open floor against *"very dense undergrowth"*
>    (*belukar*).
> 6. **Analogue against instance.** Structure, life-forms, nutrients and economics transfer;
>    climate and flora do not.

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

Compressed recall notes for the whole topic, under topic-specific headings. Every substantive
subtopic of the nineteen lessons appears. Introductory prose is not repeated; definitions,
thresholds, printed quotations, conflicts, traps, PYQ routes and answer spines are.

## Reading marks

✅ printed in a named book at the folio given · ⚠️ standard knowledge or inference, never presented
as printed · 📰 dated live retrieval with its class · ✗ a defect, quoted not asserted · ⇄ a live
conflict with both sides shown · ↪ a route-out by exact owner path · ⏱ a vintage stamp · ⛔ a refusal

## Where the type is, and the book's quarrel with itself

- ✅ Leong printed p. 116: *"The equatorial, hot, wet climate is found between **5° and 10° north and
  south of the equator**."* ⇄ ✅ Leong printed p. 114 table: Equatorial = **0°–10° N and S**, rain
  all year, **80 in**. ⇄ ✅ **Fig. 121** shades a **continuous** belt across the equator with
  **Kuala Lumpur (3°N, 102°E)** on it. **UNRESOLVED.**
- ✅ Greatest extent: the **lowlands** of the **Amazon, the Congo, Malaysia and the East Indies**.
- ✅ Latitude margin: Trades give *"a **modified type of equatorial climate with monsoonal
  influences**"*. ↪ `Geography/basic/16`.
- ✅ Altitude margin, three named places: **Cameron Highlands (Malaysia) · the Northern Andes · the
  Kenyan Highlands (East Africa)**. ✗ All three dropped by C1.
- ✅ Majid folio 5.3 gives **no latitude band**: Amazon (**selva**), Congo Basin, Madagascar, South
  East Asia (Indonesia, Malaysia, Thailand), Colombia, Ecuador, western Venezuela.
- ⚠️ The type's real edge is a **rainfall criterion** (no dry month), not a parallel. ↪ The criterion
  is Topic 14's.

## The thermal regime, measured

- ✅ *"great uniformity of temperature throughout the year"*; *"mean monthly temperatures are **always
  around 80°F**"* (**26.7 °C**); *"**There is no winter.**"*
- ✅ **Station ladder, annual range:** Quito **0.7** · Jaluit **0.8** · Djakarta **1.8** · Kuala
  Lumpur **≤2** (80→78) · Bogota **2** (59→57, **8,730 ft ≈ 2,661 m**) · Singapore **2.3** · Colombo
  **3.2** °F = **0.39–1.78 °C**. ✅ *"Over the oceans, the range is even smaller."* ⚠️ **Colombo**,
  the largest, is the most **monsoonal** station listed.
- ✅ **Verbatim, and the pivot of the topic:** *"**The diurnal range of temperature is small, and so
  is the annual range.**"* ✗ C1 asserts **diurnal > annual** four times under a book mark; **no
  comparison exists in Leong or Majid** (**C1**, **N1**).
- ⚠️ The ordering is defensible — 12-hour day (✅ Majid 5.3) gives full diurnal forcing against
  near-zero seasonal forcing — and **reverses** in continental interiors.
- ⇄ ✅ Majid 5.3: *"average annual temperature **around 25 °C**"*. Two books, ~1.7 °C apart; **not
  averaged**. ✗ C1's "26–28 °C" is a converted band (**C4**).
- ✅ Kuala Lumpur data block: **3°N, 102°E, 54 ft, 95 in**; monthly 79 79 80 80 80 80 79 79 79 79 78
  80 °F. ✅ Bogota: **8,730 ft, 63.4 in**. ⚠️ **Altitude moves the level, not the range.**

## The moisture regime and its five printed numbers

- ✅ Leong printed p. 117: *"Precipitation is heavy, **between 60 inches and 100 inches**"* =
  **152.4–254.0 cm**. ✗ C1's "150–250 cm" is a **rounded conversion** under a book mark (**C3**).
- ✅ **The criterion:** *"**There is no month without rain**, and a distinct dry season like those of
  the Savanna or the Tropical Monsoon Climates, is absent."*
- ✅ Maxima *"in **April and October**"*, *"**shortly after** the equinoxes"* — a **lag**. ✅ Minima
  *"at the **June and December solstices**"*. ✗ C1 adds *"slight"* and drops both months and minima
  (**C6**).
- ✅ Daily sequence: bright sunny mornings → much evaporation → convectional currents → *"towering
  **cumulo-nimbus**"* → afternoon *"**torrential showers**"* with thunder and lightning. ✅ *"one
  single afternoon may be as much as the **deserts receive for the entire year**"*.
- **Five values, five objects:** Leong p. 117 **60–100 in** (world type) · Leong p. 114 **80 in**
  (representative) · Majid 5.3 **about 200 cm** (ETR biome) · Khullar 168 **200 cm or more** (Indian
  generalisation) · Khullar 169 **exceeds 250 cm** (Indian formal class). ⛔ **No averaging.**

## Four rainfall mechanisms, of which the notes teach one

| # | Mechanism | ✅ Printed, Leong p. 117 | Scale | Needs heating? |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Convection | afternoon downpours from towering cumulonimbus | local | **yes** |
| 2 | **Doldrum / ITCZ convergence** | *"intermittent showers from **cyclonic atmospheric disturbances caused by the convergence of air currents in the Doldrums**"* | synoptic | no |
| 3 | **Relief / orographic** | *"mountainous regions also experience much **orographic or relief rain**"* | slope | no |
| 4 | **Equinox lag** | maxima April and October; minima at the solstices | seasonal | modulates 1–2 |

- ⚠️ **Redundancy is the argument**: only overlap produces *"no month without rain"*. ⚠️ **Doldrums
  ≈ ITCZ** is standard terminology; ✗ Leong never writes "ITCZ" and neither owner contains it
  (**N5**).
- ✅ Margin stations: **Kota Bharu, Kelantan** (NE Monsoon, end of year) · **Rangoon, Burma** (SW
  Monsoon, June–September). ↪ Beyond this line the owner is **Topic 16**, which holds **2026 Prelims
  Q33**.
- 📰 2026: reported **northward shift** in mean ITCZ position 1940–2022 and a reported **reversal
  since 1979** (*Geophysical Research Letters*, 2026). Direction and method only.

## Equability, humidity and the two modified types

- ✅ Three agents: **cloudiness · heavy precipitation · regular land and sea breezes** — *"maintaining
  a truly equable climate"*, so that *"even at the equator itself, **the climate is not
  unbearable**"*. ✗ All three absent from C1.
- ✅ *"The relative humidity is **constantly high (over 80 per cent)** making one feel **'sticky'**
  and uncomfortable."* ✗ C1 carries **no humidity figure**.
- ⚠️ **Energy currency:** desert → **sensible** heat → extreme temperature; equator → **latent** heat
  → moderate temperature, extreme humidity.
- ✅ Modification by **latitude** (trades → monsoonal) and by **altitude** (Cameron Highlands,
  Northern Andes, Kenyan Highlands).

## Rainforest architecture — serves 2021 Prelims GS-I Q60

- ✅ **Fig. 123 caption:** *"Sketch to show the **three distinct layers** of an equatorial forest"* —
  **150 feet UPPER LAYER**, **60 feet INTERMEDIATE LAYER**, with *climbing plant* and *buttress
  roots* annotated.
- ✅ **Majid folio 5.4:** *"**The rain forest canopy form three levels.**"* Upper **not continuous**;
  middle **the most continuous**; lower of **seedlings, ferns, bamboo**. ★ Two independent books,
  different wording, same count.
- ✗ **C8:** C1's **six rows** credited to books printing **three**; *under-canopy* and *shrub layer*
  have no printed counterpart; *climbers/epiphytes* is a **life-form class**, not a tier.
- ⚠️ The modern **five-stratum** scheme is standard ecology and is **never** attributed to either
  book.
- ✅ Mechanism: *"**All plants struggle upwards for sunlight** resulting in a peculiar layer
  arrangement."* ✅ *"over **150 feet**"* = **45.7 m**; ★ Khullar: *"often reach **45 metres**…
  individuals exceed **60 metres**"* — agreement to within half a metre.
- ✅ Majid: **smooth slender trunks, thin bark, buttressed by large wall-like flanks**; **no branches
  for at least the lower two-thirds**.
- ✅ **The counter-intuitive fact:** *"Because the trees cut out most of the sunlight **the
  undergrowth is not dense**."* ⇄ Khullar's Indian wet evergreen floor is a *"tangled mass"* —
  **recorded, not resolved**.
- 📰 2026: GEDI LiDAR fused with SAR gives **continuous vertical profiles** at ~25 m — "levels" are
  **modes**, not shelves. **Method only.**

## Floristics and life-forms

- ✅ **Leong p. 118 hardwoods:** **mahogany · ebony · greenheart · cabinet woods · dyewoods** — ✗ **no
  rosewood**. ✅ **Majid 5.4:** *"mahogany, ebony, and **rosewood**"*; ✅ **balsa** the light
  exception. ✗ **C7:** C1's triad is **verbatim Majid**, credited to a Leong section; Leong's three
  extras are **lost**.
- ✅ **Climbers:** *"lianas or **rattan** … **hundreds of feet long**"*; cords *"up to **20 cm in
  diameter**"* (Majid 5.3).
- ✅ **Epiphytes:** **orchids, bromeliads, ferns** — *"**supported physically but not
  nutritionally**"*. ⚠️ Leong writes *"epiphytic **and parasitic**"* — **distinct**.
- ✅ **Ground flora:** ferns, orchids, **lalang** (Leong) / seedlings, ferns, bamboo (Majid).
- ★ ✅ *"as many as **200 species of trees** may be found in an **acre**"* (Malaysian jungle,
  *"estimated"*) ‖ ✅ *"a species may occur only **once or twice per square kilometre**"*. **One fact
  at two scales** → *"not found in **pure stands**"* → *"commercial exploitation … a most difficult
  task"* → *"many tropical countries are **net timber importers!**"*
- ✅ **Fauna:** **arboreal** — monkeys, lemurs, sloths, parrots, snakes; tree frogs, lizards, bats;
  *"over **500 species of butterflies**"*. ↪ Boundary at `Env-Ecology/basic/06`.
- ✅ No seasonal synchroniser: *"some trees may be in flower while others only a few yards away may
  be bearing fruit"*.

## The nutrient paradox — serves 2023 Prelims GS-I Q63

- ✅ **Thesis:** *"**It is a misconception that tropical soils are rich.**"*
- ✅ **Qualification:** in the virgin state, *"heavy leaf-fall and the decomposition of leaves by
  bacteria"* give *"a thick mantle of humus"* making the soil *"**fairly fertile**"* — evidenced by
  *"the shifting cultivators' **heavy croppings** in their newly cleared **ladangs**"*.
- ✅ **Mechanism:** *"once the humus is used and the natural vegetative cover is removed, **the
  torrential downpours soon wash out most of the soil nutrients**"* → *"**soil erosion and soil
  impoverishment**"*.
- ⚠️ **Rate argument:** permanent warmth and moisture make decomposition **continuous** — ✅
  *"decaying do not take place in a seasonal pattern"* — so litter is consumed as it falls and **no
  deep organic horizon accumulates**. ⚠️ **High productivity + high decomposition = thin soil.**
  Contrast boreal cold-suspended decomposition.
- ⚠️ Nutrient capital sits in the **living biomass**, recycled litter-to-root → shallow rooting → ✅
  **buttress roots** are the mechanical answer.
- ✅ **Recovery: 100 to 250 years** for dominants after major disturbance (Majid 5.4).
- ✅ **Exceptions, two kinds:** **Java**, *"because of its **rich volcanic ashes**"*; and **Malaysia,
  Singapore and eastern Brazil**, by *"**systematic planning and the will of the people to
  succeed**"* — a **management** exception, routinely conflated with the soil one.
- ⚠️ **Leaching** is taught as standard pedology; ✗ Leong never uses the word (**N2**). ↪ **Laterite
  profile, horizons, chemistry, agronomy and building use are ROUTED OUT** (**C20**) to the soils
  owner, the Topic 04 lane and the Environment owners.
- ✅ Majid Fig. 5.2 (**Odum, 1971**), per 0.1 ha: temperate forest **200 trees** against grassland
  **1,500,000 plants**. ⚠️ **Not** a rain forest statistic; counts **individuals**.
- 📰 2026 Amazon: reported drought-resistant **low-water-table refugia**; a reported *Nature* (May
  2026) **range-based** transformation finding with self-reinforcing moisture-recycling loss. ⛔ **No
  single percentage as a settled boundary** (C1 §7 preserved).

## The subsistence response — ladang, belukar, lalang

- ✅ *"The equatorial regions are generally **sparsely populated**."* ✅ **Three modes**: hunters and
  collectors · forest-product collection · shifting cultivation.
- ✅ **Three named peoples and products:** Amazon **Indian tribes → wild rubber**; Congo **Pygmies →
  nuts**; Malaysian **Orang Asli → cane products**, *"sold … to people in villages and towns"* —
  **commercial**.
- ✅ **Crops:** **manioc (tapioca), yams, maize, bananas, groundnuts**.
- ✅ **The rule:** *"When the **fertility is exhausted**, the clearing is **abandoned** and they move
  on to a new plot."*
- ✅ **Belukar** = *"less luxuriant **secondary forests** … **short trees and very dense
  undergrowth**"*. ⚠️ **The "impenetrable jungle" of popular imagination is secondary forest.**
- ✅ **Lalang** = *"tall grass"* springing up when shade trees are cut; may *"**choke crops and
  overwhelm estates**"*. ✅ **Ladang** = the cleared plot. ✗ All three terms absent from both owners.
- ⚠️ **The fallow IS the fertilising operation**; sustainability is a function of **fallow length**
  alone.
- ✅ *"ever-flowing rivers and streams provide an **inexhaustible supply of fish**"*; ✅ *"**The
  rivers form the only natural highways.**"*
- ✅ Mangrove named at p. 118 and ↪ **routed** to `Env-Ecology/basic/24`.

## The commercial response, and a text read against itself

- ✅ **Three printed lumbering obstacles (p. 120):** trees *"do not occur in **homogenous stands**"*;
  *"there are **no frozen surfaces to facilitate logging**"*; hardwoods *"**too heavy to float in the
  rivers, even if these flow in the desired directions**"*.
- ★★ ✅ **Majid corroborates:** *"some species **will not even float**. Exceptions are **balsa**"*;
  species *"widely scattered … **once or twice per square kilometre**"*. **The strongest two-source
  fact in the topic.**
- ⚠️ **The chain:** many species → scattered stems → long haulage → the route is a **river** → and
  the log **sinks**.
- ✗ **C9:** *"inaccessibility"* is **not printed as a lumbering obstacle** (**N4**). ✅ Access appears
  at p. 120 **item 3** under *development and maintenance* — roads *"maintained at a **high cost**"*;
  the **Amazon, Congo and Borneo** *"without modern communication lines"*. ✅ And the **word** belongs
  to **Khullar folio 174**, about **Indian** forests: *"**inaccessible** … **non-merchantable**"*, in
  the **high Himalayas of Kashmir and Arunachal Pradesh**.
- ✅ **Five printed livestock constraints:** *"greatly **handicapped** by an **absence of meadow
  grass**, even on the highlands"* · animals *"kept mainly as **beasts of burden**"* · yields *"well
  below … the **temperate grasslands**"* · grass *"so **tall and coarse** that it is **not
  nutritious**"* · in Africa **tsetse flies** cause ***ngana***.
- ✗ **C10, FROZEN FORMULATION:** **traditional pastoralism is severely handicapped; post-clearance
  commercial ranching does occur and is a deforestation driver.** C1 line 97's absolute denial
  contradicts its own lines 63–64, 70 and 87.
- ✅ **Determinist passage (p. 118):** *"The **monotonous climate, oppressive and enervating**, taxes
  one's mental alertness and physical capability … most of the **white settlers** … take to the
  **cooler highlands**."* ✅ **C1 lines 61–64 mark it historical and not valid causal geography —
  PRESERVED.**
- ⚠️ **Split the layers:** heat-humidity work capacity ⚠️ defensible · disease burden ⚠️ defensible,
  and ✅ Leong's own *"malaria eradication schemes are in progress"* **undercuts his determinism** ·
  the capability-and-race inference ✗ **rejected**.

## The plantation overlay and four expired figures

- ⚠️ **Why it works:** woody perennials retain canopy and roots, so the soil is not bared each season
  (solves the nutrient problem); estate monoculture replaces *"no pure stands"* (solves the
  extraction problem) — at the cost of **disease exposure**.
- ✅ Estates *"especially in **Java, Sumatra, Malaysia, West Africa and Central America**"*, for crops
  *"**highly valued in the industrial West**"*.
- ✅ **Rubber:** wild collection in the Amazon (p. 118) → *"**transplanted** … grown very profitably
  on **large estates**"* (p. 119). ⏱ *"**Malaysia and Indonesia** are the leading producers, each
  accounting for **more than a third** of the world production. The home country, **Brazil exports
  practically no natural rubber**."* ✅ **Reason given:** *"**tree diseases**"* and *"**lack of
  commercial organization**"*. ⛔ **Current ranking refused** ↪ `Geography/advanced/30` (**2022 Mains
  GS-I Q15**).
- ✅ **Cocoa:** *"most extensively cultivated in **West Africa, bordering the Gulf of Guinea**"*; ⏱
  *"the **two most important producers are Ghana and Nigeria**"*; *"acreages are rapidly on the
  increase"*; the crop *"leaves West Africa for **Europe or North America**"*. ⛔ **2024 Prelims Q5
  is routed to `Geography/basic/34` and is not answered here.**
- ✅ **Oil palm:** *"**many countries outside Africa** have now taken to its cultivation."* ⏱
- ✅ **Ten-crop suitability list:** **coconuts · sugar · coffee · tea · tobacco · spices · cinchona ·
  bananas · pineapples · sago**. ✗ C1 gives four.
- 📰 2026: the **EU Deforestation Regulation**, covering **cocoa, rubber and palm oil**, reported
  postponed to **30 December 2026** (large and medium operators) and **30 June 2027** (small and
  micro), with plot-level geolocation traceability for post-2020 production. **Regulatory status and
  date only.**

## The India transfer — no Af, one shared mechanism

- ✅ **C2 preserved:** India's humid-tropical climate is **monsoonal**; its evergreen forests are
  shaped by *"**monsoon seasonality, orography and insularity**"*; India has *"**equatorial-type
  evergreen forests, not a true equatorial climate zone**"*.
- ⚠️ C2's hedge about *"some modern Köppen maps"* is carried **as C2's hedge**; ↪ the **Köppen
  criterion and Indian Köppen regions are Topic 14's**.
- ★ ✅ **The proof from Leong's own page:** **Kota Bharu**, at about **6° N**, is **inside both
  latitude envelopes** and **outside the type**, because its rain is seasonal. ✅ And his exercise
  contrasts **Alor Star** with **Bombay**.
- ✅ **Khullar folio 168 ladder:** **>200 cm** evergreen rain forest · **100–200 cm** monsoon
  deciduous · **50–100 cm** drier deciduous or tropical savana → open thorny scrub · **<50 cm** dry
  thorny scrub → semi desert. **Second axis:** above **900 m** in the Himalayas and Peninsular hills,
  *"temperature plays a more important role"*.
- ★ ✅ **Evergreen defined:** trees *"**do not shed their leaves annually, at least not together**"* —
  **de-synchronisation, not permanence** — matching Leong's unsynchronised selva exactly.
- ✅ Khullar: the dry season is *"**distinctly short**"* — which is what orographic rain achieves in
  India, and the only one of Leong's four mechanisms that transfers.

## Champion and Seth — the classification

- ✅ **H.G. Champion (1936)** distinguished **116 types**. ✅ Simplified by **G.S. Puri (1960)**,
  **Legris (1963)**, **Champion and S.K. Seth (1968)** ◄ the standard scheme, and **S.S. Negi
  (1990)**. ✅ Result: **5 main types, 16 sub-types**.
- ✅ **Criteria:** *"unequal distribution of **rainfall and temperature** as well as their **seasonal
  variation**, besides varied **edaphic and biotic** conditions."*
- ✅ **A Moist Tropical:** 1 Wet Evergreen · 2 Semi-Evergreen · 3 Moist Deciduous · 4 Littoral and
  Swamp. **B Dry Tropical:** 5 Dry Evergreen · 6 Dry Deciduous · 7 Thorn. **C Montane
  Sub-tropical:** 8 Broad-leaved hill · 9 Moist hill (**pine**) · 10 Dry evergreen. **D Montane
  Temperate:** 11 Montane Wet Temperate · 12 Himalayan Moist Temperate · 13 Himalayan Dry Temperate.
  **E Alpine:** 14 Sub-Alpine · 15 Moist Alpine Scrub · 16 Dry Alpine Scrub.
- ⚠️ **Architecture:** A and B split on **moisture**; C, D and E are successive **thermal-altitudinal**
  levels. ↪ C, D, E → `Geography/advanced/22`; sub-types 3, 6, 7 → `Geography/basic/17`,
  `/advanced/17`; sub-type 4 → `Env-Ecology/basic/24`.
- ✅ **Table 6.1 (folio 173, after Sagreiya 1994):** Wet Evergreen **5.120 = 8.0** · Semi-evergreen
  **2.624 = 4.1** · Moist Deciduous **23.680 = 37.0** · Littoral and Swamp **0.384 = 0.6** · Dry
  Evergreen **0.128 = 0.2** · Dry Deciduous **18.304 = 28.6** · Thorn **1.664 = 2.6** · Sub-trop
  Broad-leaved **0.256 = 0.4** · Sub-trop Moist Hill (pine) **4.224 = 6.6** · Sub-trop Dry Evergreen
  **1.600 = 2.5** · Montane Wet Temperate **2.304 = 3.6** · Himalayan Moist Temperate **2.176 = 3.4**
  · Himalayan Dry Temperate **0.192 = 0.3** · Alpine **1.344 = 2.1** · **TOTAL 64.000 = 100.0**.
  ✔ **Arithmetic exact, re-verified at generation.**
- ⚠️ **India's forest estate is mainly deciduous:** types 3 + 6 = **41.984 m ha = 65.6 %**; the two
  evergreen types = **7.744 = 12.1 %** of the **column total** — ⚠️ **not of India**.
- ⚠️ **U4:** no current official type-wise area table retrieved; ⚠️ **no "FSI forest types" scheme
  exists** (**G26**).

## The discriminator — wet evergreen against semi-evergreen

| | **1. Tropical Wet Evergreen** | **2. Tropical Semi-Evergreen** |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Rainfall | **exceeds 250 cm** | **200–250 cm** |
| ✅ Temperature | **about 25–27 °C** | **24 °C to 27 °C** |
| ✅ Humidity | **exceeds 77 %** | **about 75 %** |
| ✅ Dry season | **distinctly short** | *"comparatively drier areas"* |
| ✅ Density | *"lofty, **very dense** multilayered"*, mesophytic evergreens | *"**less dense but more gregarious** (especially in Assam)"* |
| ✅ Heights | often **45 m**, individuals **over 60 m** | — |
| ✅ Canopy and floor | *"green carpet when viewed from above"*; *"sun light cannot reach the ground"*; *"tangled mass of canes, bamboos, ferns, climbers"*; orchids flourish | — |

- ✗ **C11, highest-severity:** C2 attaches *"exceeds about **200–250 cm**"* to **wet** evergreen. **That
  band is semi-evergreen's.** A threshold cannot be a band.
- ⇄ **C12, UNRESOLVED:** folio **168** *"**200 cm or more** … evergreen rain forests"* against folio
  **169** *"exceeds **250 cm**"*. ⚠️ The broad-category-versus-formal-class reconciliation is the
  **reader's inference** and is **not adopted**.
- ⚠️ **Practical rule:** a *forest-type* statement matches **folio 169**; a *general vegetation*
  statement matches **folio 168**.
- ⚠️ **Temperature cannot discriminate** (25–27 lies inside 24–27). ⚠️ ***Gregarious* = purer stands =
  more exploitable** — a reversal of the equatorial *"no pure stands"* obstacle.

## Distribution of India's evergreen belts

- ✅ **Belt 1:** *"along the **western side** of the Western Ghats (**between 500 to 1370 metres**
  above sea level) **south of Mumbai**"*.
- ✅ **Belt 2:** *"a strip running from **north-east to south-west** direction across **Arunachal
  Pradesh, Upper Assam, Nagaland, Manipur, Mizoram and Tripura upto a height of 1070 metres**"*.
- ✅ **Belt 3:** *"the **Andaman and Nicobar Islands**"* — **no altitude band**; the control is
  **maritime**.
- ⚠️ Ceilings differ (**1,370** vs **1,070 m**) because the North-Eastern belt lies **further north**.
  ⚠️ The **500 m floor** reflects lowland clearance and the slope position of the orographic maximum
  — **inference**; Khullar prints the bound without a reason.
- ✅ **Semi-evergreen distribution:** **Western coast, Assam, lower slopes of the Eastern Himalayas,
  Odisha and Andamans** — *"**bordering** the areas of tropical wet evergreen forests"*. ⚠️ **Odisha
  is semi-evergreen only.**
- ✅ **Western Ghats species:** **mesua · white cedar · calophyllum · toon · dhup · palaquium · hopea
  · jamun · canes**. ✅ **Himalayan / North-East species:** **gurjan · chaplasha · jamun · mesua ·
  agar · muli · bamboo**. ⚠️ **Only jamun and mesua shared**; ✗ **not one equatorial genus**; ✅ C2:
  mahogany *"largely introduced"*.
- ✅ Timber *"**fine-grained, hard and durable**"*, *"**high commercial value**"*.
- ★★ ✅ *"**not properly exploited** due to **dense undergrowth, absence of pure stands and lack of
  transport facilities**"* — *"absence of pure stands"* is printed in **all three books**.
- ✅ **Folio 174 national frame:** about **half** exploitable, about **one fourth** potentially
  exploitable; exploitable tracts in **Assam, Arunachal Pradesh, Tripura, Western Ghats, Satpura,
  Maikal, Chota Nagpur plateau, Andaman and Nicobar, Odisha** and adjoining Andhra Pradesh and
  Chhattisgarh.
- ⚠️ **Map-bank rows ABSENT** for **Anaimalai Hills** as an entity, **Agasthyamalai**, **Malabar
  Coast**, **Agumbe**, **Mawsynram**, **Cherrapunji/Sohra**, the **Doldrums/ITCZ belt**, **Selvas**,
  **Borneo**, **Sumatra** and **New Guinea** as landmasses, **Java**, **Cameron Highlands**,
  **Kenyan Highlands**, **Northern Andes/Bogota**, and the **Western Ghats** and **Indo-Burma
  hotspots** as entities — **17 gaps declared, not closed** (**G25**).

## Reading a forest statistic — four quantities

| | ① Forest-**type** area | ② Forest **cover** | ③ Forest **cover** | ④ **Recorded** forest area |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Value | **64.000 m ha = 640,000 km²** | **677,816 km² = 20.6 %** | **715,343 km² = 21.76 %** | **775,288 km²** |
| Vintage | ⏱ **1994** | ⏱ **2003** (data period **2002**) | ⏱ **ISFR 2023**, rel. Dec 2024 | ⏱ **ISFR 2023** |
| Needs trees? | yes (classed) | yes, ≥10 % canopy, >1 ha | yes | ❗ **no** |
| Needs legal status? | no | ❗ **no** | ❗ **no** | yes |
| Source | ✅ Khullar T6.1 / Sagreiya | ✅ Khullar 174, T6.2 / SFR 2005 | 📰 search-mediated | 📰 search-mediated |

- ⛔ **No arithmetic across ① ② ③ ④.** Internal ratios **within one vintage** are permitted.
- ✅ **X5 / C15:** Table 6.1's header reads *"**Percentage of total area**"* — ambiguous. Arithmetic
  proves the denominator: **5.120 ÷ 64 = 8.0**; **5.120 ÷ 328.7 = 1.56**. ⚠️ 64 m ha is itself about
  **19.5 %** of India — a **share of a share**.
- ⚠️ **Second denominator trap:** sal **16.55 %**, teak **13.82 %**, bamboo **7.48 %** are shares of
  the **broad-leaved** area, itself **about 95 %** of forest area.
- ✅ **2003 frame:** geographical **3,287,263 km²** · world average **30.4 %** · policy **33 %**
  (**60 %** Himalayas and Peninsular hills, **20 %** Great Plains) · per capita **0.06 ha** against
  world **1.08** · Reserved **54 %** / Protected **29 %** / Unclassed **17 %** ✔ · private **under
  1 %** · coniferous **3.5 m ha** · broad-leaved **~95 %** · production **0.5** against world **2.1
  m³/ha/yr**.
- ★ ✅ **Table 6.2, nine cycles 1987–2003 with sensor and data-period columns:** 640,819 · 638,804 ·
  639,364 · 639,386 · 638,879 · 633,397 · 637,293 · **675,538** · **677,816**. **Non-monotonic**; the
  ~**38,000 km²** jump 1999→2001 coincides with the move to **IRS-1C/1D LISS III**. ⚠️ **The
  instrument changed** — the empirical reason cross-vintage arithmetic is forbidden.
- 📰 **ISFR 2023, retrieved 2026-10-04, search-mediated:** VDF (≥70 %) **99,779** · MDF (≥40, <70)
  **306,890** · OF (≥10, <40) **308,674** → **715,343** ✔ · tree cover **112,014 (3.41 %)** → forest
  + tree **827,357 (25.17 %)** ✔ · recorded forest area **775,288** · change **+156 / +1,289 /
  +1,445 km²** · mangrove **4,992 km²** ↪ routed · Lakshadweep **91.33 %**, Mizoram **85.34 %**,
  Andaman and Nicobar **81.62 %** ↪ ranking routed.
- ⚠️ **VDF ≈ one-seventh of cover**; **Open Forest alone exceeds VDF threefold**. "21.76 % forest
  cover" ≠ "21.76 % dense forest".
- 📰 **U5 CLOSED** at generation: ISFR 2023 remains the latest. ⚠️ **U3 PERSISTS**: fsi.nic.in TLS
  failure; PIB **403/401**.
- 📰 **2026 critique:** the ≥10 % canopy rule counts **plantations, orchards, bamboo, rubber and
  tea/coffee shade trees** as forest; gains reported largely **outside recorded forest areas**. ❗ **A
  canopy threshold cannot separate primary forest from belukar or from a plantation — so no national
  cover figure is ever evergreen-forest extent** (C1 lines 197–200).

## The biodiversity boundary

- ⚠️ **The test:** a **physical cause** of richness → Geography; a **conservation purpose** → Ecology.
- ⚠️ **Geography keeps:** no seasonal synchroniser → light-sorted tiering → many niches → high
  alpha-diversity with low conspecific density; and **isolation as a physical fact** — islands, a
  linear range flanked by dry lowland, the **Palghat Gap**, 2,000 km to the North-East.
- ✅ Majid 5.3: rain forests ≈ **one-half of Earth's remaining forests**, ~**7 % of world land area**.
  ✅ 5.4: *"more than **one-half of Earth's original rain forest is gone**"*, cleared for **timber,
  fuel, pastures and farming**. ⚠️ **Two different denominators — do not merge.** ★ The four clearance
  causes map exactly onto the economic lessons.
- ⛔ **Ecology takes:** hotspot criteria · conservation status · endemism as a priority category ·
  protected areas · biosphere reserves · CAMPA · REDD+ · Plan Vivo · New York Declaration.
- 📰 **May 2026:** the **Kali night frog (*Nyctibatrachus kali*)**, Kali river basin, **Castlerock**,
  Karnataka — **cryptic**, separated by **DNA and call analysis**, reportedly **micro-endemic**. ⚠️
  **Named; no conservation-status claim.** ★ Vindicates ✅ C2: *"old species counts should not be
  treated as current inventories."*

## What is contested — jhum and the ESA

- **Settled (jhum):** the **mechanism**. Fertility in biomass → burn → ash → two or three crops →
  abandon → **belukar** rebuilds the capital. **The fallow IS the fertilising operation**, so
  sustainability is a function of **fallow length**; degradation follows **fallow collapse**, not the
  technique. ✅ C2: *"sustainable when fallow cycles were long"*; jhum is not *"inherently
  destructive"*.
- **Contested (jhum):** the **numbers**. ✗→⚠️ **C19:** C2's *"15–20 years to 3–5 years"* rests on
  *"source notes kept from the earlier verified conversion"* — a **chain of custody, not a folio**.
  ⛔ **U2:** Khullar folios **176–181 rendered but uncertified**, **182–196 not rendered**; **nothing
  is quoted from either range**.
- 📰 **2026 reported:** fallows commonly **under five years**; a Mizoram district study reporting a
  dominant cycle of **about five years** and **no study area at twenty years**; a West Garo Hills
  study reporting area falling **14,984 ha (2016) → 8,391 ha (2023)**. ⚠️ **District-level, not a
  national series.** ⚠️ **Cycle length is a distribution, not a constant.**
- 📰 Reported **policy posture**: **transform rather than eliminate** jhum, traced to the **NITI
  Aayog** working-group report of **2018**.
- **Settled (ESA):** ✅ **C2 lines 77–80, preserved** — the two exercises *"used **different methods
  and landscape definitions**, so the percentages are **not directly interchangeable**"*; cycle
  lengths should be quoted *"only from a **dated local study**"*.
- ⛔ **Refused (ESA):** **no Gadgil percentage, no Kasturirangan percentage, no difference between
  them** (**U8**, refusal class 3).
- 📰 **ESA status and dates only:** a **seventh draft** reported issued **27 July 2026** across **six
  States**, open to objections; expert committee tenure reported **extended to July 2027**;
  **Karnataka** reported to have **rejected** the draft in **September 2026**, seeking a **fresh
  ground-level survey**.
- ★ **Karnataka's reported objection is a measurement objection** — the same limitation as the ISFR
  canopy critique and the *belukar* problem: **remote sensing sees reflectance, not categories.**

## The comparative spine and the answer architecture

- ⚠️ **THESIS:** **moisture quantity transfers; moisture timing does not; everything downstream of
  quantity transfers with it; the flora is independent of both.**
- ✔ **Transfers:** rainfall **amount** · **humidity** · **structure** (150 ft = 45.7 m against *"often
  reach 45 metres"*) · **life-form spectrum** · **nutrient regime** · **extraction economics**
  (*"absence of pure stands"* in all three books).
- ✗ **Fails:** rainfall **distribution** · **thermal equability** (0.4–1.8 °C) · three of four
  **mechanisms** · the **flora** (not one genus shared).
- ✗ **C14 closed:** C2's **Khullar leg verified**; its **Majid leg NOT LOCATED** (**U1**) — ⚠️ **not
  called false**. C1's grounding is **verified on both legs**, giving two independent world-side
  books.
- ⇄ **Three conflicts left unresolved by design:** 5°–10° against 0°–10°; 200 cm against 250 cm; the
  stratum count as a convention.
- ✅ **Directive decoding:** *"account for the climatic characteristics"* → mechanisms, all four ·
  *"why sparsely populated"* → constraint set against counter-cases, never determinist · *"discuss
  shifting cultivation"* → nutrient-in-biomass and the fallow · *"compare with equatorial"* → the
  nine-row spine · *"assess forest resources"* → which quantity, definition, date, denominator.
- ✅ **The 10-mark spine (C1 §9.3, preserved):** thesis of constraint-not-dictation → five constraints
  with mechanisms → four adaptations → counter-evidence (**Java**; Malaysia, Singapore, eastern
  Brazil) → modern pressure, routed → **graded conclusion**.
- ✅ **THE GRADED CONCLUSION:** **physical constraint sets the cost of occupation; technology, capital
  and institutions determine whether that cost is paid.**
